It reproduces that function deliberately, including the parts that look wrong: the `guard !vectorResults.isEmpty` that discards lexical hits when the dense arm returns nothing is preserved, because the job is predicting what the app does rather than what it should do. `run_quality_matrix.parse_stage_metrics` argues against recomputing any metric in Python, on the grounds that two implementations drift and the one that drifts silently is the one nobody tests. That objection is correct, so calibration replays the fusion at the weight the run actually used and checks it against the app's own recorded output two ways, rank order chunk by chunk and recomputed metrics against the Swift row, and refuses to emit a sweep unless both pass. nDCG is deliberately not recomputed: recall and reciprocal rank have one unambiguous definition each, and every extra recomputed metric is more surface for exactly the drift being guarded against. `--self-test` covers the fusion arithmetic against hand-computed cases, including the credited-relevance rule whose absence once made nDCG@5 report 2.131 on a metric defined over [0, 1].
- **[General]** **An evaluation pack whose ground truth this project did not write, and whose corpus contains documents that are not the answer.** `Benchmarks/ResearchFixtures/qasper_external_v1/` holds 83 cases over 40 papers drawn from QASPER (Dasigi et al., NAACL 2021, CC BY 4.0), built by `scripts/build_external_fixtures.py`. The questions were written by readers who had seen only a title and abstract, and answered by separate annotators against the full text; a case is kept only where at least two annotators agreed, and the count rides along in `annotator_agreement`. Six abstention controls come from questions a majority of annotators judged genuinely unanswerable, replacing two the project had invented for itself. The load-bearing change is not the source of the data, though: the manifest declares a top-level `pool` that `run_quality_matrix.py` ingests for **every** case, so each question is asked against 39 distractor papers. Until now the runner created a fresh store per case and ingested only that case's `input_files` (`run_quality_matrix.py:391`, `:720`), which meant every case was scored against an index holding nothing but its own expected file. The 2026-08-11 reports show the consequence directly: the vector stage saw between two and five candidate chunks per case and all of them were correct, so `R@5` and `MRR@10` read 1.000 as arithmetic rather than as a pipeline result. A fixture with no distractors can register a regression and can never demonstrate an improvement, which is why the embedder comparison had nothing to win on. `fixtures.lock.json` pins the dataset revision, the selected papers and questions, and a SHA-256 over the corpus; `--check` verifies it offline, and two independent rebuilds produce a byte-identical tree. `scripts/build_eval_dataset.py` now renders every known pack, so `Benchmarks/rag_eval_qasper_v1.jsonl` reaches the in-app `RAGEvalCase` framework the same way the synthetic set does.
- **[UI]** **Every piece of vocabulary the app puts on screen now defines itself in place, in two registers.** The onboarding completion card shows `38 TOPS`, `32/batch`, `768 search`, `16-core ANE`, `Chunks` and `Vectors` to a user roughly ninety seconds after install. Those figures belong there: "this ran on your A19 Pro at 32 passages per batch" is checkable in a way "your data stays private" is not, which is the entire reason the panel names real silicon. Unexplained, though, they read as a wall, and the one screen built to earn trust was the most likely in the app to lose it. `OpenIntelligence/UI/Components/Glossary.swift` defines **24 terms twice**: a `plain` register that uses no code identifier, model name or framework name, and a `technical` register free to name `MiniLM-L6-v2`, `vDSP_mmul`, `ms-marco-TinyBERT-L2-v2` and Apple TN3193. Definitions hang off `GlossaryTermID` through **one exhaustive `switch`** rather than a string-keyed dictionary, so a call site cannot name a term that does not exist, lookup cannot fail and returns no optional, and adding a case is a compile error until both registers are written. That shape is a direct answer to what it replaces: `InfoButtonView` took `(title, explanation)` as free strings typed separately at each of its three call sites, which is exactly how one word ends up defined two ways. The technical register sits behind a `DisclosureGroup` bound to `AppStorage`, so opening it once opens it for every definition in the app, which is what lets both registers share one screen without either being noise: a technical reader configures the whole surface with one tap, and a non-technical reader is never shown jargon at all. Wired at the point of confusion rather than into a screen of its own, because a definition in Settings is a definition nobody reads: the four pipeline capsules explain themselves **while the stage they name is the one running**, the Words/Chunks/Vectors/Time strip carries a definition per counter, all six device chips are tappable behind a dashed border, `HowItWorksView` annotates each of its four stages and links onward without restating anything, and `Settings > Device & Performance` annotates the Hardware Envelope rows plus the batch pills. `Settings > Plain English` is the index for someone looking a word up by name, not the only route in. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: Glossary.swift, GlossaryViews.swift, GlossaryView.swift, GlossaryTests.swift 14 cases]`
- **[UI]** **`GlossaryTests` treats the plain register as a claim that can rot, because it is one.** The failure mode for a two-register glossary is not a crash. It is a plain definition slowly acquiring mechanism until it reads like the technical one, at which point the app has two technical registers and no plain one, and nothing renders differently so nobody notices. Fourteen cases: a plain definition fails the suite if it contains any of 24 code identifiers, model names or framework names (`MiniLM`, `BM25`, `vDSP`, `FTS5`, `cosine`, `cross-encoder`, `threadgroup` and the rest), if it uses a backtick, if it exceeds 360 characters, or if it duplicates its own technical counterpart. The jargon list is deliberately identifiers rather than concepts: checking concepts fires on ordinary English, since `hybridSearch` legitimately says "exact words find" and `words` is also a defined term. Further cases pin that no definition contains an em-dash, that no term lists itself as related or repeats a reference, that no term is orphaned from the cross-reference graph, that every synonym returns the term it was written for, and that searching `bm25`, `vdsp`, `minilm`, `tinybert` and `fts5` still reaches the right entry, since indexing the technical register is what lets someone search the word the code uses. **Two cases guard specific hedges rather than structure.** `testTopsDefinitionDoesNotClaimAMeasurement` fails if the TOPS entry stops saying the figure is a per-chip lookup: `DeviceCapabilityService.npuTops` reads a table keyed by device identifier, several entries are explicitly projections for unreleased silicon, and Apple exposes no live Neural Engine occupancy API, so a definition implying measurement would be the same class of claim as the withdrawn `65 tok/s` and the three invented multipliers. `testNeuralEngineDefinitionKeepsTheSchedulingHedge` fails if the Neural Engine entry stops saying Core ML makes the final scheduling decision, which is the hedge `HowItWorksView` already carries and which the glossary must not be more confident than. `[evidence_level: test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: GlossaryTests.swift]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Format coverage for ingestion, which had none, asserted on extraction rather than on answers.** The automated corpus was 25 markdown files against 20 advertised formats, so PDF parsing, OCR, Office extraction, iWork and A/V had no coverage whatsoever. That gap has a measured cost, not a theoretical one: on 2026-08-08 a plausible table-chunking change destroyed **70% of retrieved table text** while the answer benchmark reported an unchanged 16/20, and it was caught only because a human noticed character counts in the running app. An answer score structurally cannot see that class of loss, so these tests do not compute one. `IngestionFormatCoverageTests` drives real files through `processDocument` for a text-layer PDF, an image-only PDF, a figures-only PDF, a partially legible scan, a PNG of a table, CSV, `.docx` and `.xlsx`, and asserts that each table row returns with its label and value **on one line**, that a multi-line page does not collapse to one line, that character yield holds against a per-lane floor derived from the characters the fixture draws, that the lane under test is the lane that actually ran, and that the chunk inventory has not changed shape, including that chunking did not lose what extraction already recovered, which is the half of the 70% incident that happened downstream of extraction. `StructuredPageContentTests` pins the `effectiveContent` merge with no fixture and no Vision call, giving that defect a deterministic guard that does not depend on OCR behaviour. **Fixtures are synthesised in Swift at test time rather than committed**: expectations and bytes derive from one `TableSpec` so there is no hand-transcribed ground truth to drift, no binary enters an iCloud-synced repository that has already broken builds by duplicating and re-stamping files, and the test target's file membership does not change, so hard-boundary `project.pbxproj` is untouched. `.docx` and `.xlsx` are built by a store-only ZIP writer in the test target, which works because the reader in `DocumentProcessor` accepts `compressionMethod == 0`; that also removes any dependency on LibreOffice being installed wherever the suite runs. Stated plainly because it bounds the claim: a page rasterised from vector text is cleaner than anything a scanner produces, so this catches **structural** regressions and not OCR accuracy loss on noisy input. All six ingestion defects it guards were structural. A real scanned corpus is still worth acquiring and this does not replace it. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: IngestionFormatCoverageTests 12/12, StructuredPageContentTests 9/9]`
- **[Retrieval]** **Per-stage retrieval metrics, because the shipped evaluation harness has never actually measured retrieval.** `RAGEvalRunner.evaluate` computes retrieval recall only when `evalCase.groundTruthChunkIds` is non-empty, and matches those identifiers against `chunk.id.uuidString`. Every one of the 20 cases in the committed `Benchmarks/rag_eval_v1.jsonl` carries `groundTruthChunkIds: null`; ground truth is held in `expectedCitations` as a **filename**, present on 18 of the 20 (the two without are abstention cases, correctly). So `retrievalRecall` is `nil` for every case, `RAGEvalMetrics.compute` calls `compactMap` on an empty set, and `retrievalRecallAt5` is reported as exactly `0.0` on every run no matter how retrieval performs. The `>= 0.85` quality gate in `meetsQualityGates` can therefore never pass. This reframes an open roadmap dispute: the fixtures are committed and generate correctly, so "the repo cannot reproduce its own quality numbers" was wrong about the cause, but the runner genuinely cannot score them. New `RetrievalStageMetrics.swift` adds recall@1/3/5/10, MRR, nDCG@5/10 and precision@5 computed **per named retrieval stage** rather than once at the end, because reranking cannot recover a chunk the first stage never returned and a first-stage regression can otherwise be masked by a later one. Relevance judging is separated into `RetrievalRelevanceJudge`, which matches on source document rather than chunk UUID. The metric math takes a rank-ordered relevance vector and references no RAG type, so it is testable against hand-worked values with no pipeline, no fixtures and no I/O; all 24 accompanying tests state their expected value's derivation in a comment rather than capturing it from a run. Aggregation preserves pipeline stage order and counts empty-result queries instead of dropping them, which would otherwise flatter a stage that fails to run. **Not yet wired into `RAGEvalRunner`**, so no measured retrieval number exists yet; this is the measurement instrument, not a measurement. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact_for_the_defect, evidence_source: RAGEvalRunner.swift:79-86, RAGEvalMetrics.swift:119-120, Benchmarks/rag_eval_v1.jsonl all 20 records, suite 173/173]`
- **[Orchestration]** **The planless routing branch now logs the decision it makes.** Commit `176e32c` changed how `FoundationModelRoutePolicy.determineRoute` estimates tokens and reads the on-device limit, but that branch emitted nothing, so the change could not be confirmed on a device. `planID: "direct"` exists only in the `ActiveModelRouteResolved` notification userInfo and all three observers drop it; `onDeviceLimit` was computed and never emitted. A device log showing an estimate beside a PCC route could not be attributed to this branch or checked against the threshold it actually compared with, which is exactly why the 4521-against-4096 log stayed inconclusive. A single `Log.debug` under category `.llm` now emits `queryType`, the estimate, `onDeviceLimit` and `pccAllowed`. No control flow changes. Note `DeveloperDiagnosticsHubView` probes the same function with a synthetic 1000 tokens and a nil plan on every body render, so lines reading `type=maximum est=1000` come from that view rather than a query, and the console `print` is `#if DEBUG` only, so a TestFlight build needs file logging with `.llm` in `fileLogCategories`. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: FoundationModelRoutePolicy.swift:71, LLMService.swift:635, DeveloperDiagnosticsHubView.swift:63]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Every image the app ingested became one unbroken line of text, and nobody could see it.** `ImageUnderstandingService` joined Vision's OCR lines with a space, then a second pass replaced any surviving newline with a space on the way into the chunk. A photographed invoice's line items, dates and amounts arrived as a single sentence: the chunker saw one paragraph, the embedding averaged the whole page, and no downstream stage could recover the structure because it was destroyed before chunking ran. Both joins now preserve line breaks. This is the silent-garbage failure rather than the loud one, ingestion reported success every time. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: ImageUnderstandingService.swift OCR join and structuredText assembly]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Photographing a document produced flat text where importing the same page produced table cells.** The camera still-capture path carried both ingestion defects at once. `CameraManager.analyzeCapture` sorted its OCR observations into reading order and then joined them with a space, discarding every line and row boundary that sort had just established, and it never reached `StructuredDocumentParser` at all. Document captures now run `RecognizeDocumentsRequest`, which parses tables and groups cells into rows, and prefer its output; non-document captures skip it because a photo of a pet has no document structure to find. Failure or an empty parse leaves the flat text untouched. API currency confirmed against Apple's documentation on 2026-08-08: `RecognizeDocumentsRequest` is current as of WWDC25/iOS 26 and nothing supersedes it, and `VNRecognizeTextRequest` is retained deliberately as the no-document-detected fallback. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+doc_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: CameraManager.analyzeCapture, developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2025/272]`
- **[Ingestion]** **A page the parser knew it had read badly was repaired, and the repair was then thrown away.** `StructuredPageContent.effectiveContent` substituted recovered raw text only when the structured parse found *no* table or list. A low-quality page that captured one table therefore kept the table and discarded everything else, including the fallback text `performTextRecognitionFallback` had already re-OCR'd for exactly this purpose. It fires on the highest-value pages: a scanned datasheet where Vision nails one table but transcribes a fifth of the surrounding prose shipped with four fifths of the page absent from chunks, from FTS5 and from the vector index, leaving a warning in the log as the only trace. It now merges, structured elements are kept and the recovered prose is appended, deduplicated against text the elements already cover. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: StructuredDocumentParser.swift effectiveContent]`
- **[Ingestion]** **A PDF with figures but no tables lost every figure chunk after paying to produce them.** `usedStructuredParsing` was derived from a counter that increments only for tables and lists, so a diagram-heavy manual reported no structure, took the flat-text branch, and discarded every structured element, titles, figure descriptions, and the embedded-image analysis chunks appended immediately beforehand. The Neural Engine time spent OCRing the labels inside each figure was spent and the output dropped before indexing. The flag now reflects whether structured extraction produced anything at all. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentProcessor.swift structured-extraction result]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Vision's document understanding ran on imported PDFs only, never on imported images or camera captures.** `RecognizeDocumentsRequest` returns cell-level table structure; the standalone-image path used `VNRecognizeTextRequest`, which returns text lines in reading order and cannot express that a value belongs to a row and a column. The same table gave cell structure inside a PDF and flat lines when photographed or screenshotted, with nothing in the app to explain the difference. Standalone images now go through the structured parser first, and its output is prepended to the existing classification and description rather than replacing them; any failure falls back to the previous behaviour, so this can add structure and never remove it. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentProcessor.extractTextFromImage, StructuredDocumentParser.parsePageImage]`
- **[General]** **Corrected the retrieval measurement itself, which was wrong in seven ways before it measured anything.** Ground truth is one document per case while retrieval returns chunks, and scoring per-chunk relevance against a per-document denominator made **nDCG@5 report 2.131** on a metric defined on `[0,1]`; a `min(1.0, ...)` clamp hid the same unit mismatch in recall, reporting perfect recall for a query whose second expected document was never retrieved. Each ground-truth document now earns credit once, at the rank of its first matching chunk. Precision divides by `k`, matching `trec_eval`'s `P` rather than `set_P`, which had inflated exactly the late stages that return fewer results and so broke the cross-stage comparison the instrumentation exists for. MRR is cut at 10 so a hit at rank 30 cannot credit a 30-result stage and not a 10-result one. `retrievalRecallAt5` is scored at depth 5 rather than over every retrieved chunk while gated at `>= 0.85`. Score ties now break on chunk id at six sites: `sorted(by:)` is unstable in Swift and several lists come from a `Dictionary` with per-process randomised iteration order, so ranks moved between identical runs, the 27 runs in `BenchmarkRuns/` predate this and are not comparable to each other. Three grader defects are fixed, all of which scored correct behaviour as failure: the engine's "could not be strictly verified" banner tripped the abstention regex; "not stated" was missing from that regex, recording a correct refusal on a negative control as a hallucination; and accuracy divided by cases that answered rather than cases attempted. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: RetrievalStageMetrics.swift, run_quality_matrix.py, suite 181/181]`
- **[Retrieval]** **Sentence extraction that matched nothing discarded the evidence retrieval had just found.** `RAGService` narrows context to query-relevant sentences for lookup-style questions. When that filter matched zero sentences it returned an empty context, and the pipeline logged "Empty context after assembly" and fell back to direct LLM chat with no RAG at all. Case `exact_capex` had the strongest retrieval in the benchmark, top similarity 1.317, the correct document at rank 1 in all five slots, MMR selecting 5 of 5 with nothing dropped, and the model received zero characters, then confidently refused. The wrong answer is the lesser problem: answering from model priors while the user believes the app is reading their documents is precisely the ungrounded behaviour the verification gates exist to prevent, reached by a path that bypasses them. Extraction that matches nothing now falls back to whole-chunk assembly. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: RAGService.swift extractive branch, BenchmarkRuns/20260808-retrieval-stages]`
- **[Retrieval]** **The per-stage retrieval metrics are wired into the harness, so the repository can measure its own retrieval for the first time.** Commit `cb82471` landed the measurement instrument and said plainly that it was not connected. This connects it, and the connecting turned out to require correcting the model of the pipeline that the instrument was written against. `RetrievalTraceCollector.Stage` assumed six stages all produced inside `HybridSearchService`, including `rerank`. **That service does not rerank.** Its only mention of the reranker is a comment about a score cap; `RAGEngine.rerank` is called later, from `RAGService`, after hybrid search has already returned. So the six stages span two layers, and a seventh was needed: `candidates`, meaning what hybrid search hands the reranker after top-K truncation and sanitising. That is a real measurement point, distinct from `boosted` because the truncation between them can drop the relevant chunk, and distinct from `final` because reranking reorders what survives. A recall drop from `boosted` to `candidates` means top-K is too tight; a drop from `candidates` to `rerank` means the cross-encoder demoted the right chunk. The instrument previously recorded its own output as `final`, which named a stage the pipeline had not reached and would have collided with the real one once reranking was instrumented. Ownership now follows where the work happens: `HybridSearchService` records `vector`, `lexical`, `fusion`, `boosted` and `candidates` in **both** its search paths, `RAGService` records `rerank` after the rerank block, and `queryWithAudit` records `final` from the chunks on the response. The `rerank` capture sits after the `if/else` rather than inside the reranking branch, because when a quality mode skips the cross-encoder the stage still describes what the pipeline handed onward, and recording only inside the branch would make the stage silently absent for Standard, the one mode with a measured baseline. The collector is threaded from `RAGEvalRunner` through `queryWithAudit`, `query` and `queryInternal` as a defaulted optional, so no existing call site changed and production pays one nil check per stage. `RAGEvalResult` gains `stageMetrics`, nil rather than empty for abstention cases so an unscored case is distinguishable from one that scored zero. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: RetrievalTraceCollector.swift, HybridSearchService.swift:283-300/1075-1090, RAGService.swift:8380-8445/9453/9892, RAGEvalRunner.swift]`
- **[Retrieval]** **`Docs/RETRIEVAL_PIPELINE.md` claimed a recall gate that had never once been met and a harness that does not exist.** Its pipeline-stages list said evaluation "verified against quality targets (e.g. Recall@5 ≥ 0.85, Citation Precision ≥ 0.90) using the native Evaluations harness". `retrievalRecallAt5` returned exactly `0.0` on every run regardless of retrieval quality, so the `≥ 0.85` gate could never pass, and `AppleEvaluationsBridge.swift` is named for Apple's Evaluations framework but imports only `Foundation` and `FoundationModels`. Corrected in place with a dated note rather than deleted, so the record shows what was claimed and when it was withdrawn, and the Mermaid flow now shows the capture points. This is the same class of claim as the Settings capability list: a document asserting a number nothing produced. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: AppleEvaluationsBridge.swift imports, Benchmarks/rag_eval_v1.jsonl all 20 records]`
- **[General]** **Claude Code sessions could not see this repository's agent directives at all.** `AGENTS.md` carries 18 binding rules, among them the `PROCEED: IMPLEMENT` gate, the fifteen forbidden-edit boundaries, and the obligation to update docs in the same turn as code. Claude Code reads `CLAUDE.md` and does not read `AGENTS.md`, and there was no `CLAUDE.md`, so a Claude Code session in this repository started with none of it: no iCloud build warning, no implementation gate, no hard-boundary list, no doc-sync obligation. A root `CLAUDE.md` of 111 lines now carries the operative subset and names `AGENTS.md` as authoritative where the two disagree. `Docs/ai/` adds what the repository genuinely lacked rather than restating what it already has: `STATE.md` is a cross-session handoff carrying the current objective, what has actually been verified and by which command, and one exact next action, so a fresh conversation continues without the prior transcript. `PROJECT.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md`, `DECISIONS.md` and `RUNBOOK.md` sit beside it and route into the existing `Docs/` set rather than duplicating the Atlas or the canonical source of truth. Six rules in `.claude/rules/` are path-scoped and therefore cost nothing at session start: the hard-boundary rule fires when one of the protected files is opened, and the retrieval, ingestion, orchestration and user-facing-copy rules restate the doc-sync table from `.agents/rules/01-docs-and-notion-sync.md` for the paths being edited. Three skills (`project-orient`, `project-handoff`, `project-context-audit`) and three hooks complete it: a bounded SessionStart brief that reports branch, working tree, and whether `STATE.md` has fallen behind HEAD; a PreCompact checkpoint that never blocks compaction; and a Stop check that asks for one handoff pass. The Stop hook compares against a baseline written at session start and fires at most once, so a read-only session is never blocked. `AGENTS.md` is deliberately restated rather than imported with `@AGENTS.md`, and the drift risk that choice creates is recorded with its reasoning in `Docs/ai/DECISIONS.md`. Route `repoos_workspace_automation` now covers `.claude/**`, `CLAUDE.md`, and `Docs/ai/**`, which it previously did not. `[evidence_level: doc_verified+code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: code.claude.com/docs/en/memory "Claude Code reads CLAUDE.md, not AGENTS.md" fetched 2026-08-07, Claude Code 2.1.220, hook smoke test, preflight re-run reporting the new allowed paths]`

- **[General]** **The task router read a roadmap section number as the app's version, and told every agent to write releases against it.** `repoos_router.py` derived the active release by scanning candidate documents with `^##\s+(\d+\.\d+)`, and `Docs/ROADMAP.md` was first in that list. That file numbers its *sections*, `## 0.`, `## 0.5`, `## 1.`, `## 2.5`, so the router matched `## 0.5 Instrumentation & Benchmarking` and reported `v0.5` as the active release with `confidence: exact`. Confidently wrong is the worst failure mode available to a governance tool: every preflight handed out a wrong `[Unreleased]` target, a wrong release-notes section, and a wrong Notion `Target Release`, and `.agents/workflows/sync-notion.md` explicitly instructed agents to take `Target Release` from that field. Its own valid-options list stopped at `v4.6`, so a v5.0 item would have been written as a nonexistent `v0.5` and then "rounded to the closest valid option", landing on `v4.0`. Derivation now comes from `CHANGELOG.md` alone, which is the only version marker the build reads: `ci_scripts/ci_post_clone.sh` stamps `MARKETING_VERSION` for both platforms from its first `## <number>` heading. **Reporting that heading is not sufficient either,** and the reason is already written in that CI script: when `[Unreleased]` holds entries, the heading beneath it names a version already cut, which is exactly what made CI stamp an already-released 4.6 and got the build rejected by App Store Connect on 2026-07-28. So the release is now three facts rather than one, `version`, `state` (`shipped` or `in_development`), and `last_shipped`, with the target taken from a `<!-- next-version: 5.0 -->` marker beside the `[Unreleased]` heading, invisible to the CI grep because it needs a digit right after `## `. With no marker the router reports `unreleased` at `confidence: unknown` rather than guessing. The suite went from 11 of 14 to 19 of 19, and the three failures were themselves the defect's cover: they asserted the literal `v4.6` against the live working tree, so they broke when 4.7 shipped and stayed red through 4.8 and 4.9 while the real bug sat underneath. They are rebuilt against throwaway fixtures, plus a regression test asserting a `## 0.5 Some Section` heading is never read as a version, and a live check that compares against the file instead of a literal. A test that asserts a moving value against the working tree is a calendar, not a test. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: repoos_router.py, ci_scripts/ci_post_clone.sh:23-71, suite 24/24, unreleased-entry count parity with the CI shell verified on identical rules]`
- **[General]** **The mandated Notion roadmap workflow named tools that do not exist, and Claude Code could not have read it anyway.** `.agents/workflows/sync-notion.md` is binding under `AGENTS.md` rule 14, and it specified `API-query-data-source`, `API-post-database-query`, and `API-retrieve-a-database` with Notion filter-object bodies. The connected `mcp.notion.com` server exposes `notion-query-data-sources`, `notion-fetch`, `notion-update-page`, and `notion-create-pages`, and its query tool takes **SQLite against the data source URL used as a table name**, not filter objects. So all three documented tiers would have failed as unknown tools, and the stop-on-failure rule would then have halted every roadmap sync. Separately, `.agents/` is Antigravity's directory: Claude Code never loads it, so the recipe was unreachable from Claude Code regardless of whether it was correct. The verified recipe now ships as a `notion-roadmap` skill, written from calls actually run against the live database rather than from documentation, and the `.agents/` copy is corrected and cross-referenced. Its `Target Release` list, which had gone two releases stale at `v4.6`, is re-read off the live data source. `[evidence_level: verified_by_execution, confidence: exact, evidence_source: live query and page-creation calls against data source 37f49a74-d54f-81b0-92d9-000bce5e05fa, 2026-08-07]`
- **[General]** **Scoped this repository's MCP surface to what it actually uses.** Supabase (`execute_sql`, `apply_migration`) and Docusign (`createEnvelope`) were reachable in every session here with no connection to an on-device iOS RAG app. Both are denied at the server level in `.claude/settings.json`; Notion stays, because the roadmap depends on it. The deny rules name connector ids rather than names, because connector tools carry no readable server name, and those ids are per-install: if a connector is removed and re-added the rule silently stops matching, with no startup warning, because ids contain `_`. The mapping and the re-derivation step are recorded in `Docs/ai/RUNBOOK.md` so the rot is detectable. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: .claude/settings.json, connector tool ids observed in-session 2026-08-07]`
- **[UI]** **The shared pipeline trace now reports the library's index state, so a device defect can be diagnosed from a shared file instead of an attached Xcode console.** A query that returns nothing because retrieval found nothing relevant, and a query that returns nothing because the library has no vectors at all, produce the same answer on screen and read identically in every section of the exported trace. The line that separates them is logged under `Log`'s `.vectorDB` category, and `.vectorDB` is not in `fileLogCategories`, so it never reached `pipeline_trace.log` and only ever appeared in a console attached over a cable. Three open defects are told apart by exactly that distinction and all three are device-only, which is why they have stayed open. The trace gains a `LIBRARY STATE` section carrying the container, its document count, the ingested chunk count and the searchable one side by side, whether a rebuild is flagged, and the stored embedding fingerprint against the live one. Both chunk counts are reported rather than one, because `totalChunks` records what ingestion believed it wrote while `count()` records what retrieval can reach, and a library once read 420 against a vector store holding none. The snapshot is read when the trace is shared rather than captured with the message, since the interesting state belongs to the store and not to the answer, and it is deliberately side-effect free: it does not route through `resolveEmbeddingContext`, which flags and can wipe a store, so sharing a trace cannot alter what the next trace reports.
- **[UI]** **Tapping the pipeline trace button crashed the app.** `SwiftUICore/EnvironmentObject.swift:93: Fatal error: No ObservableObject of type RAGService found.` Introduced the same day by the change that added the `LIBRARY STATE` section: `MessageBubbleV2` reached for `RAGService` with `@EnvironmentObject` after confirming `ContentView` injects it, without checking that this view tree receives it. It does not. `ChatScreen` takes `ragService` through its own `init` as an `@ObservedObject`, so the object has never been in the environment on the chat path, and `@EnvironmentObject` traps on absence rather than returning nil — which turns a missing section into a crash. The dependency is now threaded explicitly, the way every other callback in that view already travels: `ChatScreen` supplies a `libraryStateProvider` closure from the `ragService` it holds, `MessageListV2` forwards it, and `MessageBubbleV2` calls it only if present. Every level defaults to nil, so a host that does not supply it yields a trace without a `LIBRARY STATE` section rather than a crash — the same fail-safe rule the snapshot itself follows when a vector store read fails. It also repairs the SwiftUI preview, which the environment version would have crashed on sight.
- **[Orchestration]** **Deep Think turned 2,305 words of evidence into an eight-word answer, and every stage logged success.** Observed on device 2026-08-19, the first execution of the source-only verifier anywhere. Three defects stacked. **Intent:** `investigatePatterns` already contained `what affects` and `what influences`, so the shape was recognised, but the mechanism verbs were missing — `what regulates`, `what controls`, `what modulates` and five more now join them. Without that, "What regulates anxiety-like actions?" fell to the `lookupStarters` prefix rule, was classified as a spec-value lookup, and opened `isExtractiveFirst` — the single gate on both ExtractiveQA and the verifier. ExtractiveQA then scored `behaviors.56` and `Neuron 2016` as part numbers before giving up at 0.50 against a 0.65 threshold. **Budget:** the verifier's evidence was bounded by a hardcoded 6 records x 420 characters, which at `onDeviceCharsPerToken` 1.4 is 1,800 tokens against a measured budget of 1,430 — so the evidence could not fit by construction and records were dropped on every full-evidence run rather than as an exception. The capture dropped 4 of 6 and cut the candidate answer from 4,051 to 268 characters, leaving roughly 6% of what retrieval found. Snippets are now sized against the budget, so all six records survive shorter rather than two surviving whole: on those numbers 6 x 233 characters instead of 2 x 420, which is more total evidence with every source still represented. **Consistency:** the review step was handed the untrimmed evidence while the extraction step received the trimmed set, so the two stages disagreed about what evidence existed and the reviewer could ground claims in records the drafter never saw. The caller rejects claim ids it does not recognise, which the capture logged seven times as `unexpected claim id C2..C8; ignored`. Both stages now read the same records. Also removed: a `words.count <= 5` fallback that claimed extractive intent purely from brevity. Two further defects were found while fixing these and are pinned by failing-on-purpose tests rather than changed — a bare `what` prefix still implies lookup, and `computePatterns` contains the substring `sum` so `summary` and `summarize` are classified as arithmetic before the summarize branch is reached.
- **[Orchestration]** **Deep Think cited `[S13]` and `[S17]` against a twelve-source list, and nothing caught it.** Four defects, from a device capture on 2026-08-19. **The citations were correct and the source list was wrong.** The reasoning chain labels sources by global position in `routedChunks` (see `labelOffset`), and 20 chunks entered the pipeline, so `[S13]` was a valid reference. But the two reasoning-chain returns in `AgenticOrchestrator` handed back `allRetrievedChunks` — the pre-routing accumulation, 12 items — while `initialSources:` three lines above already passed `chainResult.routedChunks` with a comment explaining that labels index into it. Both returns now report the array the chain actually labelled. **The detector was pointed at the wrong text.** `verifyCitations` has an explicit `Source index out of range` branch that never fired, because it only inspects the final answer while the dangling citations were in the reasoning trace — which is rendered to the user and included in the exported trace. A `danglingCitations` check now runs over the reasoning trace and logs rather than renumbering, because a dangling citation means the labels and the source list have diverged and silently renumbering would hide that. **Half the citations could fail and the answer still shipped.** The gate was `groundingScore < 0.3`, so seven of ten citations could be ungrounded; the capture logged `accept (relevance=54%, citations=2/4, confidence=67%)`. Now a strict majority must check out. A first attempt used `< 0.5` and would still have accepted the exact 2/4 case, which the new tests caught before it shipped. **Confidence could not express failure.** A third `completeness` term worth 30% of the score was computed from answer length and source count, both of which saturate — length at 500 characters, sources at five — so it returned 1.0 for every real answer and handed over its full weight regardless. With the 0.30 floor, a completely ungrounded and completely irrelevant answer reported 30% confidence. Confidence is now relevance and grounding only, weighted evenly, and the floor is 0.05. Removing just the length half was not enough and still produced exactly 0.30; the test caught that too.
- **[Retrieval]** **The lexical arm's best hits can no longer die before the reranker sees them.** Per-case forensics (2026-08-20, `BenchmarkRuns/LEDGER.md`) established that the dense arm returns essentially the whole corpus unthresholded, so RRF fuses 3–9 precise lexical hits into a corpus-length list and a lexical rank-1 gold chunk can leave fusion below the order-dependent top-K cut — in one measured case gold sat at lexical rank 1 of 3 and never reached the cross-encoder at all. `guaranteeingLexicalSurvivors` now unions the top-K cut with the lexical arm's best hits (capped, tail-appended — the cross-encoder scores the whole pool regardless of position), in both hybrid paths. Measured paired on 7 identical cases with the lexical control bit-identical: rerank r@5 and r@10 rose 0.714 → 0.857 — the cross-encoder surfaced gold it previously never received — and the baseline's only zero-recall case flipped to a correct answer (4/7 → 5/7). Costs one case a within-top-3 shuffle (final MRR 0.786 → 0.679 at n=7, noise scale) while final r@10 held at 0.857. Device-corroborated on the 2026-08-19 regression query: the survival log fired and the eight-word answer became a grounded 4,593-character one.
- **[Indexing]** **Four ways a library's vectors could die while its metadata survived — the phantom-library data loss, closed at every path found.** Reproduced live on device 2026-08-20: a freshly imported library (197 searchable chunks, answering Deep Think queries) came back after relaunch as `1 document(s) and no vector store yet`, the document invisible. Four holes, all fixed: **(1) memory-pressure eviction** removed cached stores without persisting them — a store whose writes were still buffered (persistence is deliberately deferred for batching) lost those chunks with nothing on disk, and on-device generation is exactly when memory warnings fire; eviction now persists first and refuses to evict anything that fails to persist. **(2) The config-mismatch invalidation** dropped stores the same way when a dimension or kind changed; it now persists the outgoing store before recreating. **(3) The deleted-mid-import fallback** in `dbForContainer` silently persisted a document's vectors into whichever library was on screen when the target id failed to resolve — observed doing exactly that during a delete/re-ingest session, where the persisted store belonged to no container the sync sweep could find; it now throws a named, user-visible error (`VectorStoreRoutingError.containerNotFound`) so a failed import is retryable instead of a phantom discovered days later. **(4) Backgrounding** checkpointed SQLite and saved the transcript and ingestion queue but never flushed the vector stores; `persistAllVectorStores()` now runs first in the background transition. Suite-verified; the closing evidence remains on-device — the phantom not re-forming across real use, and any lookup failure now surfacing as the thrown error naming the missing container.
- **[Indexing]** **A library detected as broken repaired itself only if you happened to visit the right tab.** `evaluateSemanticIndexHealth` logged `Semantic retrieval cannot work here; surfacing a rebuild` and then only inserted into `librariesNeedingIndexRebuild` — a set with exactly one reader in the app, the banner in the documents list. A library detected while the user was in Chat therefore stayed broken: the query returned `Fused 0 results into 0 unique chunks`, and the owner's device capture of 2026-08-20 contains no self-healing line anywhere, because nothing enqueued one. The detector now calls `enqueueSelfHealingRebuild` directly, which is idempotent (guards on suppression, pending set, queued item and running task) and still honours an earlier user dismissal by re-publishing the banner instead of repairing. It runs on every evaluation rather than only the first, so a library already flagged in a previous session cannot return early forever and never attempt repair.
- **[Ingestion]** **Ingestion could deadlock while resolving a file path, stalling the whole queue.** Captured by stack sample on 2026-08-21 from a benchmark case sitting at 0.0% CPU: `runIngestionLoop → addDocument → generateQuestionsForIngestedDocument → mergeIntoPersistedBank → loadQuestionBank → AppSupportPaths.suggestedQuestionsURL → baseDir → OpenIntelligenceRuntimePaths.baseDirectory → objc_sync_enter → _os_unfair_lock_lock_slow → __ulock_wait2`, blocked for the entire sample window. The main thread sat idle in its run loop, which is why the app looked alive at 0.1% CPU rather than spinning, and why this read for months as a mysterious per-document stall. `waitForIngestionCompletion` then blocks on an item that can never finish, so one path lookup halts the queue. Every acquisition in that file was correctly paired with a release — the flaw was the primitive: `objc_sync_enter` takes an Objective-C object and selects a lock from a global striped table keyed by pointer, so unrelated objects sharing a stripe share a lock, and a Swift enum metatype is not an Objective-C object. The three override properties now use a dedicated `NSLock` that nothing outside that file can contend, with filesystem work kept strictly outside the critical section. In benchmark runs this presented as an intermittent 1800s timeout roughly once per eight cases, previously and wrongly attributed to specific documents.
- **[Retrieval]** **Spec-rescued chunks were displacing the cross-encoder's top result.** `filteredChunks.insert(contentsOf: boostedRescued, at: 0)` put rescued chunks in front of the best reranked chunk, and `applyMMR` selects its first chunk by position, unconditionally — as does the MMR-skipped path, which takes `prefix(effectiveTopK)`. The careful scoring three lines above (`rescueScore = topScore - 0.02`, with a comment stating rescued chunks must rank *below* the top result and 'won't displace genuinely high-relevance content') was therefore decorative: position decided, and every rescue evicted the reranker's #1. Measured over 25 cases as `rerank` r@1 0.667 collapsing to `final` r@1 0.417. Rescued chunks now insert after the top result; they still enter the candidate set and still reach MMR. Verified paired on 24 cases with the lexical control identical: `final` r@1 0.417 → 0.500, MRR 0.590 → 0.646, correct 9/24 → 11/24. Part of the loss remains — `final` r@1 is still below `rerank`'s 0.667 — so at least one other stage between reranking and the answer is still dropping rank-1 chunks.
- **[General]** **Retrieval can now be scored at passage level instead of document level.** Every retrieval figure this project has produced credits a whole document when any of its chunks appears, and on 2026-08-21 that ruler produced four wrong conclusions in one day — the last of them inverted, where injecting a document summary raised `r@1` (a summary *is* a chunk of the gold document) while making the answer worse, because a summary cannot answer an extractive question. 19 of 25 QASPER cases are `answer_kind: extractive`, so the answer is a literal span and "did the span reach the model" is the question that matters; it could not previously be asked, because `retrieved_chunks` was an integer count and chunk text was persisted nowhere. The debug validation harness now emits a `RETRIEVED CHUNK TEXT` block of every chunk handed to the model, and the quality matrix scores `passage_present` and `passage_chunk_rank` by sliding a 12-word window over the fixture's `expected_evidence[].excerpt`. Cases without an excerpt, and reports predating the block, return `None` rather than a miss — an unmeasurable case must never look like a failure. Existing metrics are untouched so historical runs stay comparable. Debug-harness only; not on any production path.
- **[General]** **Passage scoring reported short gold excerpts as missing rather than unmeasurable.** QASPER's `expected_evidence` sometimes carries a section heading ("Experiments ::: Automatic Evaluation Metrics" — four words once normalised) instead of a prose sentence. The matcher required six words and skipped anything shorter, so a case whose every excerpt was a heading fell through to `passage_present: false`. The first partial run surfaced three cases scored "span absent, answer correct", which is impossible and is what exposed it. Short excerpts are now matched whole, four words is the floor, and a case with nothing measurable returns `None` — which is what the function's own documentation had promised. Verified against five unit cases covering present, absent, too-short, no-excerpt and no-chunk-block.

### Removed
- **[General]** **Deleted three Core AI files that were scaffolding, not integration.** `Services/AIPlatform/CoreAI/` held `CoreAIEmbeddingBackend` (`generateEmbedding` returned `[]`), `CoreAIExecutionBackend` (`execute` returned `[:]`), and `CoreAIModelRegistry` (a dictionary whose `registerModel` was never called, carrying a `case reranker` that nothing implemented). None of the three imported Apple's `CoreAI` framework and all three had zero callers repo-wide. They were unrelated to the real integration in `Services/Embedding/Providers/CoreAISentenceEmbeddingProvider.swift`, which does `import CoreAI` and uses `AIModel` and `InferenceFunction` against a bundled `.aimodel`. The name collision actively misled: a directory called CoreAI containing an unimplemented reranker case made the Core AI reranking integration look missing rather than never started. A fourth file of the same kind survives and is tracked rather than deleted, since it has a real replacement pending: `Services/Evaluation/AppleEvaluationsBridge.swift` is named for Apple's Evaluations framework, does not import it, returns `[String: Any]` dictionaries, gates on iOS 18, and carries a placeholder comment referencing a `LanguageModelEvaluation.Metric` API that does not exist. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: repo-wide caller search, suite 173/173]`

### Changed
- **[UI]** **The onboarding completion card now says that its figures are tappable.** The prompt under the spec chips read "Tap any figure above for what it means, or see every word", which named the destination rather than the affordance: "every word" describes the glossary sheet, and nothing indicated that `A19 Pro`, `45 TOPS`, `48/batch` and the rest are each individually interactive. It now reads "Tap any figure or spec for its definition, or open the full glossary", which states the two available actions in the order a reader can take them. The accessibility label moves from "Explain these words" to "Open the full glossary", and the hint now mentions that each figure can also be tapped on its own, so a VoiceOver user learns about the per-chip affordance that sighted users discover by trying.

### Fixed
- **[General]** **Release notes had drifted into three different shapes across versions, and the user-facing docs hadn't been touched since 2026-08-19 despite 20+ fixes landing after that.** Wrote a standing structure rule (`.claude/rules/user-facing-copy.md`) so future releases stop drifting: `-` bullets always, never `*`, one blank line between sections, section names either a short noun or "Your <thing>" for content that's the user's own. Applied it to the entire v5.0 entry, including normalizing 57 bullets that had been using the old `*   ` marker within this same release. `Docs/USER_CHANGELOG.md`, `OpenIntelligence/Resources/VersionHistory.md`, `WHATS_NEW.md` and `WhatsNewStore.swift` now cover the vector-loss fixes, the ingestion deadlock, the citation and fusion fixes, both UI fixes from today, and the glossary expansion — all previously undocumented outward-facing. `Docs/RELEASE_NOTES.md`'s self-tracked entry count, which had been stale at 161 for four days, is recounted to 217. `[evidence_level: build_verified, confidence: exact, suite: 277 tests 0 failures]`
- **[UI]** **The glossary's `vector` entry stated one embedding provider's dimension count as if it were universal.** The technical register said "384 dimensions... from MiniLM-L6-v2" unconditionally; `AboutView.swift` shows the app switching live between four embedding providers by `settings.defaultEmbeddingProvider`, at 384D (the default), 512D, or 1024D. Corrected to name all four and point at where a user can see which one is active for a given library. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: AboutView.swift:41-49]`
- **[UI]** **Seven glossary terms added, covering jargon the app shows constantly with no definition anywhere: quality modes, self-checking, routing, confidence, fidelity, verification gates, and the abstained badge.** Found by auditing every Settings screen, the chat response UI, and the Atlas/Database tabs against the 25 terms that existed, prompted by the owner noting the glossary read as generic for how large the app actually is. The most load-bearing finding: `confidence` (a continuous 0-1 score, 50% relevance / 50% grounding) and the new `fidelity` entry (Source-Locked / Partially Supported / Not Enough Evidence, read from the gating decision's category) are two genuinely different measurements shown in the same response-details screen with nothing explaining that they can disagree, or why. Also corrected the existing `citation` entry to state that `[S1]`/`[S2]` inline markers and the numbered source chips share one numbering system, which was previously implicit. A new `trust` glossary section ("How you know an answer is right") holds confidence/fidelity/verificationGates/abstained; the three answering-mechanics terms (qualityMode/selfRAG/routing) joined the existing `answering` section. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: SettingsView.swift quality-mode feature list, AgenticPolicyService.calibrateSelfRAGConfidence, SourceFidelityStatus.swift, ResponseDetailsView.swift:227-238, VerificationGatesOverlayView.swift:29-64]`
- **[UI]** **The Plain English glossary now opens definitions the same way every other tappable term in the app already does, instead of trying to make `List` push navigation coexist with `.searchable`.** `GlossaryView` pushed `GlossaryTermDetail` via `NavigationLink(value:)` on a `List` that also carried `.searchable(text:)` — the entry above's fix moved the destination registration off that list and the bug survived, which places the cause in the transition itself rather than in registration scope: `.searchable` installs a `UISearchController` on the same navigation controller a push has to animate through, and the two visibly raced. Rather than keep guessing at that interaction, the row now opens `GlossaryTermSheet` — the exact `.sheet(item:)` presentation `GlossaryInfoButton` already uses successfully from `HowItWorksView` and every Settings hardware row — which sidesteps the collision instead of trying to out-maneuver it. `GlossaryTermSheet` carries its own isolated `NavigationStack`, so tapping a "Related" term inside the sheet still pushes correctly, independent of this screen's search bar. `[evidence_level: code_verified + reuses_a_working_code_path, confidence: high, still_unverified_on_device_or_simulator: the Claude Code iOS Simulator panel remained down; reported by the owner with an exact repro on a physical device]
- **[UI]** **Switching to or from an empty library made the whole library picker jump back to the first pill and the screen flash.** `libraryContentView` swaps its entire content between `emptyStateView` and `documentListView` with a plain `if/else` depending on whether the active library has documents, and `ContainerPickerStrip` — the horizontal library picker — was reached through `documentHeader`, called independently from *both* branches. SwiftUI cannot tell that a `documentHeader` on one side of that branch is "the same" view as a `documentHeader` on the other; they are different parents, so crossing the empty/non-empty line tore the entire header down and rebuilt it from scratch, including the picker's own `ScrollView`. A freshly built horizontal `ScrollView` starts at its leading edge, which is library 1's pill, even though `containerService.activeContainerId` never actually changed underneath. `documentHeader` now renders once, above the branch, in `libraryContentView` itself, so it survives switching between an empty and a non-empty library instead of being torn down. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentLibraryView.swift, reported by the owner tapping between libraries]`
- **[UI]** **Opening a definition from the Plain English glossary could fail to land, and the back button could leave a blank screen behind.** `GlossaryView`'s `List` carried both `.searchable(text:)` and `.navigationDestination(for: GlossaryTermID.self)` on the same view — a known collision point, since `.searchable` and `.navigationDestination` both install machinery on the surrounding navigation controller, and a push fired from inside a searchable list can animate without resolving against a destination registered on that same list. `.navigationDestination` now lives one level up, on a plain `Group` wrapping the searchable list, which the search controller has no reason to touch. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: standard_fix_for_a_known_SwiftUI_collision, unverified_on_device_or_simulator: the Claude Code iOS Simulator panel was down for the duration of this fix; reported by the owner tapping into glossary terms]` **RETRACTED, same day, confirmed insufficient on a physical device.** The owner's precise repro — tapping a term animates without showing new content, the *next* back tap is what reveals the term detail, and a second back tap is what a correct single pop should have done — is a transition running one step behind the real navigation stack, not a destination-registration scope problem. Moving where `.navigationDestination` was declared could not have fixed that. See the next entry for the actual fix.
- **[General]** **One truncated character killed a five-hour benchmark arm.** `run_quality_matrix.py` read the app's stdout through `subprocess.run(..., text=True)`, which decodes strictly. On 2026-08-22 the v4.9 baseline arm died at case 11 of 83 with `UnicodeDecodeError: 'utf-8' codec can't decode byte 0xef in position 50103: unexpected end of data` — the app's output was cut mid-multibyte-glyph, the exception raised out of `subprocess.run`, and the remaining 72 cases were lost along with the overnight window. The 5.0 arm had completed only because it happened not to emit a split character. Both long-output calls now pass `errors="replace"`; the report is parsed with regexes over ASCII markers, so a replacement character costs nothing and a run can no longer be lost over the encoding of a log line. `[evidence_level: observed, confidence: exact, evidence_source: /private/tmp/shipcfg-49.log]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **The App Store listing claimed Private Cloud Compute works, and no shipped build has ever contained it.** Established 2026-08-21 by reading the Xcode Cloud workflow: `Default` builds with **Xcode Version = "Latest Release", currently Xcode 26.6 (17F113)**, and Xcode 27 is offered there only as the alias "Latest Beta or Release — Xcode 27 beta 5". Every release was therefore produced by Xcode 26.x, which compiles out the eleven `#if compiler(>=6.4)` sites across six files, so PCC is absent from the binary and the entitlement surviving distribution signing was necessary and never sufficient. **The app itself was already honest** — `FoundationModelCapabilityProvider` returns `supportsPCC: false` on that toolchain and the UI reads that snapshot, so nobody was offered PCC and then failed. The defect was entirely in outward copy, and exactly one claim was unhedged: promotional text reading "Answers run on your device, or on Apple Private Cloud Compute with your permission." That clause is removed; the App Store description now says the support is built and will enable in a future release rather than implying it is live. Two true README claims were **scoped rather than deleted**, per the claim-audit rule: the 86 tokens/sec PCC measurement and "confirmed on a physical device" are both real and were both obtained from a local Xcode 27 build, which is now stated. The in-app reason string changed from "PCC requires iOS/macOS 27" to name the build instead of the OS, because on an iOS 27 device the old wording contradicted what the user's own phone was running. No PCC code was removed; it lights up when the workflow moves to Xcode 27. `[evidence_level: read_from_app_store_connect + code_verified + vendor_docs_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: Xcode Cloud workflow screen, FoundationModelCapabilityProvider.swift:90-101, developer.apple.com/news/releases]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **The in-app trace export contained no engine log output at all, so testing on a phone meant testing blind.** `PipelineTraceExporter` was assembled entirely from the UI's `capturedThinkingEvents` (`ChatScreen.swift:2769`), which is a summary of what the screen already displayed. It carried no retrieval internals, no LLM detail, and **no ingestion whatsoever**, since ingestion happens at import time and belongs to no message — so the one artifact a user can produce without a Mac was structurally unable to show an ingestion defect. A bounded in-memory ring now captures the same lines the console prints and the export appends them as `▶ ENGINE LOG`. Bounded on **both** line count (4,000) and bytes (512 KB) rather than either alone, because ingestion emits thousands of lines per document (5,245 measured in one session) so a line cap still permits an unbounded share, while a byte cap alone permits unbounded array growth. Capture sits outside `#if DEBUG` deliberately, since the console does not exist on an untethered phone; the existing level and category gates still apply and what the app *logs* is unchanged. Correcting a claim made in this entry's first draft: a Release build contributes **nothing** by default rather than "errors only", because `_enabledCategories` is empty in Release and the category guard runs after the level guard, so a categorised `.error` is dropped as well. It is not permanently dark either, which the first draft also got wrong: Settings -> Developer is **not** `#if DEBUG` gated, ships in Release, and sets `currentLevel` and `enabledCategories` at runtime, so this buffer does fill on TestFlight once logging is turned on there. Guarded by its own lock rather than `stateLock`, which `writeToFile` holds across the file write. Nine tests cover ordering, both caps, eviction keeping the newest, and 500-way concurrent logging. `[evidence_level: test_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, unverified_on_device]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **Trace rotation ran once per launch and then never again, so it fired at the worst possible moment.** The size check sat *below* `if let handle = _fileHandle { return handle }` in `traceFileHandle()`, so it was evaluated on the first log line after launch and skipped for every line after. Within a session the file grew without limit; the check then fired on the *next* launch — which is exactly when someone relaunches the app to go and share a trace, moving the session they wanted into `pipeline_trace.prev.log` and leaving a nearly empty live file. Observed directly on 2026-08-21: a 569 KB `.prev.log` beside a 145 KB live one. Rotation is now evaluated against an in-process byte counter, so it costs no `stat` per line, and the fresh file's header names the previous file. `[evidence_level: code_verified+observed, confidence: exact]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **Trace lines were being spliced into one another and truncated mid-word.** `FileHandle(forWritingTo:)` plus `seekToEndOfFile()` keeps a per-handle file offset, so two handles over the same path each believe they own the end of the file and overwrite one another. Measured at ~0.2% of lines in a real simulator trace, 14 of roughly 6,900, one of them cut off mid-token at `[CoreMLSentence` with a second entry's timestamp spliced into it — rare enough to read as a formatting quirk and in fact destroyed evidence. The trace is now opened `O_APPEND`, which makes seek-to-end and write a single atomic kernel operation so concurrent writers interleave between lines instead of inside them. This deliberately does not depend on identifying which writer was responsible; the guarantee holds for all of them. `[evidence_level: measured+code_verified, confidence: exact_for_the_defect, mechanism_not_isolated]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **Shared traces truncated chunk text at 300 characters, which made them unable to answer the question they were most often exported for.** Measured chunk lengths in this app run to about 2,600 characters, so 300 discarded roughly nine tenths of every chunk. On 2026-08-21 that invalidated an analysis attempting to determine whether the answer-bearing passage had actually reached the model: the passage was usually past character 300, so it read as absent whether it was present or not. The cap is now 4,000, which covers the longest chunk observed with headroom, and when it does truncate it reports the full length rather than a bare ellipsis. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: exact]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **The documented way to get a device trace off a phone did not exist, and neither did the undocumented one.** `LoggingConfiguration.swift` has told readers to run `scripts/pull_trace.sh` since the file logger was written and **that script was never in the repo**; `UIFileSharingEnabled` was also unset, so `Documents/pipeline_trace.log` was invisible in the Files app. Two routes to the only on-device evidence artifact, both dead, which is why every request for device evidence has actually been a request to sit tethered to Xcode. `UIFileSharingEnabled` is now set (the only Info.plist change, made under explicit approval; `LSSupportsOpeningDocumentsInPlace` was deliberately *not* added since it changes document handling rather than visibility) and `pull_trace.sh` exists, handling device and simulator, fetching `pipeline_trace.prev.log` alongside the live file, and failing with the three things actually worth checking rather than a stack trace. Verified end to end against a booted simulator: 1,406 and 5,520 lines pulled. `[evidence_level: test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: scripts/pull_trace.sh run against 8FA2B3CE]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **Documented what the trace file is and is not, after an audit answered a question that had been open for months.** The Xcode console is a **superset** of `pipeline_trace.log`, not a different view of it: both are written by the same `Log.log()` call, but the file is filtered to six categories while the console is filtered to none, and `pipelineStep`/`pipelineHeader`/`pipelineComplete`/`section`/`box` are console-only. Only the `▶ QUERY:` / `◀ QUERY COMPLETE:` separators exist in the file and not the console. So a tethered console capture is strictly the better artifact and always was. What neither carries is the in-app share, which is built from the UI's `capturedThinkingEvents` (`ChatScreen.swift:2769`) and contains **no `Log` output at all**, so no ingestion. And a Release or TestFlight build emits nothing anywhere: `#if DEBUG` around `print`, an `.error` default level, an empty category set and `_fileLogEnabled == false` are four independent gates and all are closed. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Spatial PDF extraction asked PDFKit where every word was using a range that drifted off the word.** `extractTextWithSpatialOrdering` built the `NSRange` it hands to `page.selection(for:)` from a hand-maintained counter that under-counted two independent ways, and the two compounded: `split(whereSeparator:)` omits empty subsequences, so every run of whitespace collapsed into one gap while the cursor advanced by exactly `+ 1` per gap, and `String.count` counts grapheme clusters while `NSRange` addresses UTF-16 code units, so every ligature drifted it further. Both errors under-count in the same direction, which is why the range stayed **in bounds** and PDFKit kept returning bounds, for different text than the word being positioned. The word appended to the line was correct and the coordinates recorded against it belonged to something else, so nothing threw and no log fired. The range now comes from the `Substring` itself, `NSRange(word.startIndex..<word.endIndex, in: pageString)`; `in: pageString` is load-bearing, since `in: word` would give word-relative offsets and reintroduce the same defect in a new shape. Seven tests pin the arithmetic across whitespace runs, ligatures, accents and astral characters, and one asserts the old counter **cannot** satisfy the invariant, so a future simplification back to a counter fails rather than regresses quietly. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentProcessor.extractTextWithSpatialOrdering, SpatialOffsetArithmeticTests]`
- **[Ingestion]** **A page whose columns could not be detected was silently read across the columns, and non-deterministically.** When `detectColumnBoundaries` returns empty, `extractTextWithSpatialOrdering` treats the page as a single column and sorts lines by Y alone. Genuinely single-column pages and multi-column pages whose boundaries were missed both land there and are indistinguishable from inside the function, and in the second case a Y-only sort interleaves the columns by construction, because a line on the left and a line on the right at the same height sort adjacent. It then returned non-nil, so nothing downstream could tell. Separately, `sorted(by:)` is not stable in Swift, so lines at equal Y came out in arbitrary order and the same page could extract differently on two runs. Ties now break on X ascending, which is deterministic and, on an undetected two-column page, at least reading-order-correct per row, and the branch logs itself. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, symptom_attribution_still_open]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Every silent fallback to raw page text now says so, which is why the two-column defect had to be diagnosed by inference.** Both call sites read `extractTextWithSpatialOrdering(from: page) ?? text`, where `text` is raw `page.string` and interleaves columns by construction, so a nil return is a materially worse result rather than an equivalent one, and there was no way to distinguish it from a success in any trace. The batch path now logs the selected `PageProcessingStrategy` per page and whether spatial extraction succeeded or fell back, the `spatialLines.count > 3` bail warns with the line and unplaceable-word counts, and the second call site warns when it falls back. This is instrumentation, not a behaviour change: it exists so the next device trace can attribute the remaining damage instead of the next reader inferring it. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact]`
- **[Ingestion]** **The app advertised Pages, Numbers and Keynote support in five outward-facing places while every such import threw.** The in-app half of this was corrected earlier in 5.0, in the sample corpus and the picker's format list, but the README, the App Store description, `PROJECT.md`, the picker's caption and the error string itself still promised iWork support, and the error string called it "limited" when it is zero. Verified before removing, per the claim-audit rule: `extractTextFromIWorkDocument` inspects directories only, so a single-file `.pages` (what Pages on iOS always writes) never reaches a content read, and for the package form it looks for members with extension `xml` or `txt` while modern iWork stores content as compressed protobuf in `Index/*.iwa`. Also checked the other direction, that the claim is not merely unverified: the iOS 27 SDK on this machine exposes **no** iWork text-extraction API, and the QuickLook route out is closed too, since `QLThumbnailGenerator` has no page-index parameter and `QLPreviewController` is UI-only, so a headless render reaches page 1 and no further. The formats stay in the picker deliberately, so the failure is reachable and explained rather than hidden. The App Store listing changes only when the metadata is pushed to App Store Connect. `[evidence_level: code_verified+sdk_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentProcessor.extractTextFromIWorkDocument, iPhoneOS.sdk framework sweep]`
- **[Indexing]** **The two embedding providers were never comparable in a single run, so no retrieval figure knew which one produced it.** Core AI runs `main.mlirb`, which takes `input_ids` only and returns `last_hidden_state[:, 0, :]` — the CLS position. Core ML runs the `.mlpackage`, takes `attention_mask`, and is mean-pooled in Swift. `all-MiniLM-L6-v2` is trained for mean pooling, so the two paths produce genuinely different vectors from identical weights, and the choice between them was invisible from settings. `EmbeddingService.forProvider` now honours a `benchmarkEmbeddingProvider` user default, set only by `DebugRAGValidationHarness` from a launch argument and absent in a shipping app, so one benchmark run can hold everything else fixed and vary only the provider. Paired over the 21 comparable cases the swap moved `vector r@1` from 0.000 to 0.571, 12 cases better and 0 worse, exact two-sided sign test p = 0.0005. MiniLM was never the weak component; it was being read at the wrong position.
- **[Indexing]** **Benchmark runs were merging their fixture documents into the owner's real ingestion queue.** `mergeIngestionQueueIfNeeded` resolves `localRoot` through `applicationSupportRoot()`, which does not consult the storage override, so every run wrote fixture files into the live queue. The visible symptom was a "Resume interrupted upload?" prompt listing fixture filenames on every launch, which survived being discarded because the next run wrote it again. The guard stands the merge down entirely while `OpenIntelligenceRuntimePaths.areOverridesPinned` is set. Fixed at the caller rather than inside `applicationSupportRoot()` on purpose: that function is also resolved by four `coordinated*` iCloud primitives and by `BNNSVectorDatabase`, so teaching it the override would silently redirect live iCloud sync into a temporary directory. A caller that must not run during a benchmark asks; the path does not lie to everyone else.
- **[General]** **The quality matrix can pin the embedding provider and the sampling mode.** Without both, a provider comparison silently varied two things at once, and a temperature comparison run under greedy sampling returns a null result that means nothing. `scripts/run_quality_matrix.py` now threads the provider override through to the harness and records it in the run manifest, so a run's provenance is readable from its own artifact rather than reconstructed from shell history.
- **[Indexing]** **The sync service's vector-store work is now visible in a trace.** A device capture recorded **432 `[BNNS] Loaded` and 210 `[BNNS] Persisted`** lines in a single boot, across three stores holding 26, 182 and 1451 chunks — the 1451-chunk store was read 88 times and written 44 times in a session where nothing changed. The bursts are periodic rather than random: roughly 40 loads per ingestion-queue restore and 20 per scene-phase change. `mergeVectorStoresIfNeeded` opens three databases per container per pass (two in `loadVectorChunks`, one for the merge) and writes one, and `BNNSVectorDatabase.init` reads from disk, so each pass is real I/O. These constructions bypass `VectorStoreRouter`, which is the only thing that caches them. Solving 3 opens + 1 write per merge against 1 + 1 for `persistVectorChunks` fits the measured 432/210 exactly at 111 merge passes and 99 standalone persists, but an arithmetic fit is not a measurement. This change is **instrumentation only, with no behaviour change**: entry and exit counters on the merge pass, and a line naming each store opened. The file previously carried three log statements in total, which is why the churn went unattributed — its absence from a log was never evidence it had not run.
- **[Orchestration]** **The source-only verification prompt had one unbounded input, and it overflowed the context window.** Observed on device 2026-08-18: after a 5-session reasoning chain, 11 steps and 132.5 seconds, `SourceOnlyAnswerService` failed with `Draft generation failed: The session's transcript exceeded the model's context size`. The stage meant to produce the answer failed at the last moment and the run continued, so from outside it read as a slow success. **The filed diagnosis was wrong and is corrected here:** the session is constructed fresh, so nothing accumulates across the chain. Every input to that prompt is already bounded — `buildEvidenceRecords` caps at 6 chunks of 420 characters and the instructions are a constant — except `candidateAnswer`, which is whatever the chain produced, 455 words in that capture and able to be far longer. The prompt is now sized against `FoundationModelTokenBudget` before it is built, with allowances for the output, the guided-generation schema (which reserves context on top of the prompt) and tokenizer disagreement. The candidate answer is trimmed at a sentence boundary first, since it is the unbounded input, and only if the evidence alone still does not fit are records dropped from the tail. Both are logged, because a silent cut is the defect this pipeline keeps reproducing.
- **[Indexing]** **A test that pins the two embedding providers to the same answer.** `EmbeddingProviderAgreementTests` embeds identical text with Core AI and Core ML and asserts cosine > 0.99, on the reasoning that both run the same MiniLM weights and, after the mean-pooling re-export, should also pool the same way. This is the check that validates the re-export: confirming the exported graph declares an `attention_mask` input proves the graph's shape, not its numbers. A second case asserts two unrelated short texts stay apart, because the signature of padding dominating the mean is that short inputs converge regardless of content. **It skips in the simulator and cannot be made to run there**: `CoreAISentenceEmbeddingProvider` resolves its model resource at line 78 and sets `isModelLoadingFailed` when that lookup fails, before any load is attempted, while a device capture shows `Loaded Core AI model successfully` and `available: true`. The tokenizer loads in both places; the model only loads on hardware. The suite is therefore 238 tests with 2 skipped, and a skip here means unverified rather than fine.
- **[Indexing]** **The Core AI embedding model is re-exported with mean pooling, which is what the model was trained for.** `all-MiniLM-L6-v2` is a mean-pooling model; the Core AI export took `last_hidden_state[:, 0, :]`, the CLS token, using a position the model was never trained to make meaningful as the entire representation of a chunk. The vectors were not garbage — 384-dimensional, normalised, stable, passing every integrity check — they simply encoded far less than they should, which looked like a weak embedder rather than a misconfigured one. Measured over 21 paired QASPER cases the difference is `vector r@1` **0.000 → 0.571**, 12 cases better and 0 worse, exact two-sided sign test **p = 0.0005**. The export now averages token embeddings over a real attention mask, and `CoreAISentenceEmbeddingProvider` passes that mask; the mask is load-bearing rather than decorative, because every input is padded to 512 and without it the average is diluted by up to 511 `[PAD]` vectors so short texts collapse toward each other.
