Verified on the artifact: `strings` on the new `main.mlirb` lists `input_ids`, `attention_mask` and `embeddings`, where the previous committed artifact listed only `input_ids` and `embeddings`. **The vectors themselves are not yet verified** — that needs a cosine comparison against the Core ML provider on identical text, or the 25-case benchmark, and correct Core AI should *match* correct Core ML rather than merely beat CLS. This deliberately lands after the fingerprint, so existing libraries detect the change and surface a rebuild instead of silently mixing two generations of vectors.
- **[Retrieval]** **The agentic retrieval path now records per-stage metrics, so Deep Think's recall gap can be attributed instead of guessed at.** The first benchmark pairing the modes found standard at `final` r@10 **1.000** and deep-think at **0.625** on the same eight cases — and since `correct == (gold_recall == 1.0)` held in 14 of 15 scored runs, that gap *is* the quality difference between the modes. Standard reported all seven stages; deep-think reported one, because `executeFullRetrievalPipeline` took no `trace` parameter and every agentic query goes through it. Two candidate causes were refuted by reading source within the hour — expansion does not degrade the lexical arm, and it does not replace the original query, because `searchWithFTS5` searches `originalQuery` and treats expansions as a fallback only. Rather than guess a third, the path is instrumented: `executeFullRetrievalPipeline` accepts a collector, and `queryWithAudit` holds it for the query's duration so all nine agentic call sites inherit it without a signature change. Diagnostic only, nil in production, and deliberately not concurrency-safe — noted at both sites. **No retrieval behaviour changed.**
- **[UI]** **The 5.0 What's New sheet describes 5.0.** It still listed eight ingestion defects from early in the cycle and said nothing about the embedding fix, the sync loop, the Deep Think speedup, the launch work or the interface pass — the release had moved on and the sheet had not. It now leads with the index reading the wrong part of the model, which is the one item that asks something of the user (their libraries will offer a rebuild once), and compresses the four separate ingestion entries into one because they were four ways of saying the same thing. Headline updated to cover all three arcs rather than only ingestion, and matched across `WhatsNewStore.swift`, `Docs/USER_CHANGELOG.md`, `WHATS_NEW.md` and the bundled `VersionHistory.md`, which that file's own comment requires. Deliberately **no accuracy figure**: `vector r@1` 0.000 to 0.571 is real but measured on QASPER fixtures, and a paired benchmark the same day showed answer accuracy is bounded almost entirely by retrieval, so quoting a synthesis-side number to users would overclaim.
- **[General]** **The benchmark harness reaps the app on timeout, instead of leaving it to poison every later case.** `subprocess.run(timeout=)` kills the process it started and nothing else, so a timed-out case left an `OpenIntelligence` instance resident, holding the shared library. On 2026-08-19 a paired run completed case 1 in both modes (257s and 197s) and then timed out at 1800s on **every** case from 2 onward — standard mode included, which had just answered case 1 in under 200 seconds. Two app processes were still alive at 0% CPU four and a half hours later. One scored pair survived out of eight. The wider damage is that **timing from any run containing a timeout is suspect**, since a case may be measuring how long it waited for a predecessor rather than its own work. The timeout handler now reaps, the run start asserts a clean slate and warns if it finds one, and both match `Contents/MacOS/OpenIntelligence` rather than the build path — a pattern like `oi-mac-40.*OpenIntelligence` also matches the harness itself, whose command line contains `--app`, and that mistake ended the same run manually at case 11 of 16.
- **[Indexing]** **The sync short-circuit's own comparison now checks cheap keys before payload.** `vectorChunksAreIdentical` walked one chunk at a time through id, documentId, content, parentContent, contextualPrefix and all 384 floats. It now compares identity and sizes across the whole array first, then the expensive payload only for survivors, with embeddings compared by `memcmp` rather than element-wise through a generic `==`. Same semantics for IEEE bit patterns, one memory compare per chunk instead of 384. **This fixed nothing that was broken** — it was written believing it caused a 4-7x benchmark regression, and there was no regression: the baseline runs used `pool_limit: 10` and the comparison run used `pool_limit: 40`, so 269s/case against 925s/case is roughly linear in corpus size and exactly what the configuration costs. It is kept because the cost genuinely scales with library size and the cheap form is correct for large libraries regardless. `BenchmarkRuns/LEDGER.md` carries the full arc, including the refuted hypothesis and the field-name error that produced it.
- **[General]** **The test suite can run on a physical device, which it never could before.** `OpenIntelligenceEngine.framework` is built with an absolute macOS install name, `/Library/Frameworks/OpenIntelligenceEngine.framework/OpenIntelligenceEngine`, and is embedded nowhere. The app does not link it so the app runs fine; the test bundle does, and on device failed at load. In the simulator that path resolves, which is why 238 tests pass there and **zero** had ever run on hardware — every "device-verified" claim in this repository until now came from reading console output by hand. `scripts/run_device_tests.sh` embeds the framework into `OpenIntelligenceTests.xctest/Frameworks`, where the bundle's rpath already pointed, rewrites both install names to `@rpath`, and re-signs. No project change, so no hard-boundary approval; the real fix is `DYLIB_INSTALL_NAME_BASE` and is tracked separately.
- **[Indexing]** **The mean-pooling re-export is verified on hardware.** `EmbeddingProviderAgreementTests` passed on an iPhone 16 Pro Max: Core AI and Core ML agree at **cosine > 0.99** on identical text across a two-word, a five-word and a 160-character probe, and two unrelated short texts stay below 0.95 apart. The first proves both providers now pool the same way from the same weights — impossible before the re-export, when Core AI read the CLS token. The second is the attention mask specifically: without it, padding dominates the mean and every short input collapses toward the same point, which no dimension or norm check can see. This is the `vector r@1` 0.000 → 0.571 fix, confirmed rather than argued.
- **[Orchestration]** **Deep Think dropped from 279s to 80.3s on the same query and library, and returned a longer answer.** Device capture 2026-08-18: `Corpus exhausted after 5 distinct window(s); capping below the 8 sessions this mode allows`, five sessions run, zero repeats, 512 words out against 450 before. The three removed sessions were byte-identical re-reads, so nothing went unread. Measured with the Foundation Models Instruments template, generation is 90% of a Deep Think query's wall clock (48.7s of 54.2s across 8 generations on the Mac build), which means session count was the only meaningful lever and there is no orchestration overhead left to reclaim.
- **[Indexing]** **Libraries now carry a fingerprint of the pipeline that produced their vectors, so a changed embedder is detectable.** The staleness check compared `embeddingProviderId` and `embeddingDim`, and both are *declared* values — `actualProviderId` returns a string literal, `outputDimension` returns a number from a hardcoded table. Neither reads an artifact, so neither moves when the artifacts move. Commit `2753d15` changed two bundled `tokenizer.json` files, altering every vector the app would produce, and left both sides of that comparison equal; its own message says "nothing detects the change". The coming mean-pooling re-export would do it again. `EmbeddingFingerprint` hashes what actually decides the numbers: provider, dimension, max sequence length, pooling recipe, model revision, chunker recipe, and **a byte hash of the resolved `tokenizer.json`** — the term that would have caught `2753d15` with no developer discipline at all. Memoised, because the check runs on every query and hashing 711 KB is a millisecond well spent once and wasted thousands of times. Deliberately **not** a hash of the compiled `.mlmodelc`, which is `coremlc` output and can change on an Xcode bump with byte-identical numerics — that would rebuild every user's library on an app update that changed nothing. **The response is a flag, not a wipe.** A dimension change makes old vectors incompatible and wiping is right; a content change makes them stale — still 384-dimensional, still searchable, just worse — so this surfaces the rebuild banner and leaves a working library working until the user chooses. A container with no fingerprint recorded adopts the current one silently rather than being flagged, because nil means unknown: it may predate the field, or an older device may have stripped the key on an iCloud round trip through `mergeContainersIfNeeded`.
- **[Indexing]** **The 43 MiB embedding model no longer loads before the first frame.** `MLModel(contentsOf:configuration:)` is synchronous and ran inside `CoreMLSentenceEmbeddingProvider.init`, reached from `ContentView.init` by way of `RAGService`, so every launch paid for it whether or not anything was ever embedded. Four device captures on 2026-08-18 recorded launch hangs of 2.94s, 3.75s, 4.31s and 4.06s, and in none of those sessions did anything embed — the cost bought nothing. It now loads on first use, so a session that never ingests and never queries never loads it at all. Three things this needed beyond moving the call. **A lock**, because the class is a non-`Sendable` `final class`, `embed` runs at `embeddingConcurrency > 1`, and Swift 5 language mode will not diagnose N concurrent first-callers each loading their own 43 MiB copy. **A corrected `isAvailable`**, which read `model != nil` — equivalent while the load was eager, but with it deferred that reports unavailable until something first embeds, and `EmbeddingService.forProvider(allowFallback:)` routes on it, so a cold app would silently fall back to a different provider and produce a library embedded by two different models. It now tests for the resource in the bundle, which is the honest question and costs a lookup rather than 43 MiB. And **`disableIngestionMode` only restores a model that was actually loaded**, since reloading there would reintroduce the cost on a path the user cannot see. Both ingestion-mode switches now share one model factory with the lazy load instead of carrying their own copies of the lookup.
- **[Orchestration]** **Deep Think re-read the same text three times and charged you 105 seconds for it.** The chain builds one context window per session upfront, sliding through the retrieved chunks. When the corpus ran out it reset the offset to zero and kept building until it had as many windows as the mode allows — 8 for Deep Think. A 20-chunk library at 4 chunks per session yields **5 distinct windows**, so offsets went 0, 4, 8, 12, 16 and then 0, 4, 8 again. A device capture on 2026-08-18 shows it plainly: sessions 6, 7 and 8 logged the same sentence counts, source counts, character counts and starting labels as sessions 1, 2 and 3. Three of eight generations, roughly 35 seconds each, in a query that took 279 seconds. Two changes were needed, because fixing one alone does nothing: window building now stops when the corpus is exhausted, and the session loop runs `min(maxSessions, distinctWindows)` rather than the mode's maximum — otherwise the surplus sessions would simply all re-read the *last* window instead of the first three, since `contextIndex` clamps with `min(sessionIndex, count - 1)`. **No evidence is skipped.** Every chunk that was read before is still read; only the repeat passes are gone, and a repeat pass cannot introduce evidence the model has not already been given. **The confidence-based early exit is deliberately untouched.** It requires confidence, sub-question coverage and novelty exhaustion together, and loosening it to stop on confidence alone would be unsafe in an app where a Self-RAG pass has accepted a self-contradicting answer at 88%.
- **[Ingestion]** **An import could write its document to one library and its vectors to another.** `addDocument` captures its target container once at the top and uses that captured id for the document record, the FTS5 rows, Spotlight, the entity index and the stats. For the vector store alone it called `dbForActiveContainer()`, which re-reads `containerService.activeContainer` at the moment of the call. An import runs for as long as extraction, OCR and embedding take — minutes for a large PDF, and the device capture shows one at 1 hr 14 min — so switching libraries while it worked sent the document one way and its vectors the other. The result is a library holding a document, its chunk count and its full-text rows with no vector store at all: it lists in the Database tab, shows nothing in Atlas, and answers nothing in chat, which is exactly the state observed on device as `Container 009866A3 has 1 document(s) and no vector store yet`. Both ingestion paths now resolve the store by the captured id through a new `dbForContainer(_:)`. `RAGService+Streaming.importLargePDFStreamed` already took `activeContainerId` as a parameter and used it for every other write; the vector store was the one place it read the live value instead, and being the longest-running import path it had the widest window to diverge. Read-only callers that legitimately want whatever library is on screen — the visualization and analysis paths — still use `dbForActiveContainer()`.
- **[Indexing]** **A library that lost its vector store could not repair itself, and never even noticed.** Reproduced on device 2026-08-18: a document was listed in the Database tab, Atlas showed nothing for it, and querying fused `0 results into 0 unique chunks` and hard-exited. No rebuild banner appeared and no self-healing ran. Deleting the library and re-importing was the only way out, which is what the owner did. Three causes, all fixed. **The veto is now stage-aware.** `reembedDocuments` built its exclusion set from *every* `ingestionItems` entry with no stage filter, so a finished `.complete` item vetoed its own document forever; `pruneCompletedIngestionItems` bails whenever any item is non-terminal, so one stuck `.paused` entry pinned every completed entry and each pinned entry blocked a repair. There is also an everyday trigger with nothing stuck: `pruneCompletedIngestionItems()` defers four seconds while `kickPendingReembedIfNeeded()` fires immediately after, so freshly imported documents vetoed their own re-embed. **A blocked rebuild now throws instead of returning normally.** An empty rebuild had two very different causes reported identically: a library with no documents is a genuine no-op, but a library that has documents and still rebuilt nothing was vetoed — and returning normally told the caller it had succeeded, so it logged success and cleared the banner having rebuilt nothing, and the next health check re-flagged it. That cycle does not terminate. `RAGServiceError.rebuildBlockedByQueue` now distinguishes them and the self-healing caller re-inserts the banner rather than reporting success. **Health is checked when a query comes back empty.** `evaluateSemanticIndexHealth` had one caller, gated on the active container actually *changing*, so a library that broke while the user was already in it was never examined — which is why nothing surfaced. It now also runs when retrieval returns zero chunks, detached so it cannot slow the failing query and gated so it costs a store count only in the case that has already failed.
- **[Indexing]** **The workspace sync was rewriting every vector store on every pass, and each rewrite triggered the next pass.** A device capture on 2026-08-18 recorded 96 store opens and 48 rewrites in a single boot, from **one scene change and zero container changes** — six full passes nothing the user did can account for. Roughly 200 MB written per boot for zero content change, about half of it queued to iCloud as replacement uploads. Two independent fixes. **`synchronizeVectorStore` skips a write whose destination already holds exactly that content.** Both stores are read into memory a few lines earlier, so the comparison costs no extra I/O and is exact rather than heuristic; it compares every persisted field including the full embedding, so a re-embed that keeps chunk ids still writes, which is the case an id-only or count-only check would silently skip. Consolidation passes, where `sourceContainerIDs` names more than the target container, are excluded and write unconditionally as before. **`coordinatedMergeData` no longer writes identical bytes, and no longer posts `.localWorkspaceDidChange` when it did not write.** That was the loop: `workspaceSignature` hashes the file content-change date, so an identical-bytes rewrite still moved the date, still incremented `observedWorkspaceChangeCount`, and still triggered the next pass. All four transforms already serialise with `.prettyPrinted` and `.sortedKeys`, so identical content really does produce identical bytes and the guard fires in practice rather than merely existing.
- **[UI]** **The source chips under an answer had the same gesture defect as the library chips.** `SourceChipsView` tracked press state with `.onLongPressGesture(minimumDuration: 0, pressing:)` on a `Button` that already carried `.buttonStyle(.plain)`, inside a horizontal `ScrollView`. A SwiftUI gesture there competes with UIKit's scroll pan with no way to arbitrate between them, so a press that drifts a few points either scrolls or registers, unpredictably. Press feedback now comes from a `ButtonStyle` reading `configuration.isPressed`, which is the button's own recognizer and is arbitrated by UIKit against the scroll view, so scrolling wins until the press resolves. The visual is unchanged: identical 0.95 scale and 0.1s easeInOut. A repo-wide sweep confirms this was the last instance of the pattern.
- **[Orchestration]** **Leaving the Chat tab while an answer was streaming cancelled it and threw the partial text away.** `ChatScreen`'s `.task(id: activeContainerId)` restarts on every appearance, not only when the container changes, and its first statements cancelled the in-flight query and called `resetStreamingState()` unconditionally — above the `isSameChatContainer` guard three lines below that computes exactly the right condition. Switching to Documents to check a filename and coming back left an idle composer with no partial answer, no message and no error. The explicit Stop button preserves the partial answer, so the accidental path destroyed more than the deliberate one. The cancellation now sits inside the guard, where the rest of the task's per-container work already lived: history reload, suggested-question invalidation and count recalculation were all gated that way and only this block was not. Cross-container bleed protection is unchanged, because a container change is the only case that can produce it. Scene-phase cancellation and manual stop are untouched.
- **[UI]** **The transcript stopped stealing your place when you left the tab.** `onAppear` scrolled to the newest message unconditionally, and it fires on every return to Chat, so scrolling up to re-read an answer and glancing at another tab threw the position away — visibly, since `animated: false` still routes through a 0.12s `withAnimation`. It now jumps only on first open, or when the reader was already at the bottom. `isPinnedToBottom` was already maintained from the bottom-anchor preference for exactly this decision and simply was not consulted. Streaming auto-follow is untouched; the three `onChange` handlers do that work and already gate on the same flag.
- **[UI]** **Atlas stopped hiding a finished profile behind a spinner.** `analyze()` sets `isAnalyzing` before doing any work and the body rendered the loading card whenever it was true, so re-entering the tab replaced a complete in-memory profile with a spinner: the page collapsed, the scroll position clamped to the top, and everything reflowed once the identical result was re-derived. The card now shows only when `currentProfile` is nil, so a library's first analysis still displays it and a refresh over existing data keeps the old profile on screen until the new one replaces it.
- **[UI]** **A streaming answer no longer re-parses itself on every tick.** `MessageListV2` handed the whole accumulated `streamingText` to `MarkdownText` each pump tick, and `MarkdownText.body` calls `MarkdownParser.parse` as its first statement — 14 whole-string ICU substitutions, roughly two regex evaluations per line, and an `AttributedString(markdown:)` per paragraph. The pump runs at 80ms and tightens to 20ms under backlog, so that was 12.5-50Hz of O(length) parsing on the main actor, during the interaction a user watches most closely, with every intermediate result discarded milliseconds later. The streaming bubble now renders plain `Text` and the identical `MarkdownText` takes over the moment the stream closes, so the finished answer is formatted exactly as before and partial markdown no longer flickers as half-open `**` and fenced blocks resolve. **The per-parse cost is unmeasured** — the capture this came from carries no timings — so confirm the magnitude with the SwiftUI instrument's Long View Body Updates lane on a Release device build rather than assuming one.
- **[UI]** **The Atlas tab built its profile twice on every entry.** A bare `.task` and a `.task(id:)` both called `refreshAnalysis()`. The duplicate could not be cancelled away either: `analysisTask?.cancel()` cannot stop the in-flight copy because neither `buildProfile` nor `analyzeTopics` checks `Task.isCancelled`, and `LibraryVisualizationEngine` is `@MainActor` throughout, so full-text tokenisation of every chunk held the main actor for the duration, twice. `.task(id:)` already fires on first appearance as well as on container change, so removing the bare one covers both cases the pair covered.
- **[UI]** **The Documents toolbar is five direct icons with no overflow menu.** `Add Document`, `Semantic Search`, `Library Settings`, `Remove All Documents` and `Delete <library>` were previously two text capsules plus an ellipsis hiding the other three, so half the actions cost an extra tap and the row still overflowed. All five are now single glyphs on one row, plus `Cached Documents` when the cache has anything in it. Titles move to `accessibilityLabel` and `help`, so VoiceOver announces the full name including which library a destructive action targets. Icon-only chips take a 44pt minimum touch target despite looking smaller, because an unreliable tap is indistinguishable from a slow app. The two destructive actions are separated from the constructive ones by the `Spacer`, their glyphs deliberately do not rhyme, and only library deletion is tinted red — a row of identical red icons is how someone taps the wrong one. Losing the visible text costs the on-screen indication of which library is affected; that is recovered by the existing confirmations, which already name the library and state what survives.
- **[UI]** **Press-and-hold on a library chip fought the scroll view it lives in.** The chip carried a manual `onLongPressGesture(minimumDuration: 0.45)` feeding a confirmation dialog, inside a horizontal `ScrollView`. SwiftUI cannot arbitrate that against UIKit's scroll pan, so a press that drifted a few points either scrolled or opened the dialog, unpredictably, with no press affordance to indicate which was happening. Interactive glass made it worse by being switched off precisely when the chip had actions (`interactive: !hasLibraryActions`), so the chips a user needs to press were the ones that did not respond to pressure. Press-and-hold is now `.contextMenu`, which UIKit arbitrates itself: scrolling wins until the press resolves, and the preview, threshold haptic and standard animation come for free. That block already existed as the macCatalyst implementation and is now the only one, so both platforms present the same actions in the same order.
- **[UI]** **"New Library" suggested a name that already existed.** The default was `"Library \(containers.count + 1)"`, which counts rather than numbers. A workspace holding General, Library 2, 3, 5 and 6 has a count of 5 and was offered "Library 6", because Library 4 had been deleted — and nothing downstream rejected it, so accepting the suggestion produced two libraries with the same name, separable only by UUID. `ContainerService.nextAvailableLibraryName()` now takes the highest existing `Library N` suffix and scans forward until the name is genuinely free, case- and whitespace-insensitively so "library 6" counts as taken. Both the suggestion and the empty-name fallback use it. Separately, the destructive action read "Delete the Library 6 Library"; it now reads `Delete “Library 6”`.
- **[Orchestration]** **Deep Think was answering from a fraction of what it retrieved, and the part it discarded included the best chunk.** `executeDirectSynthesis` ended in `String(searchResults.prefix(3000))`, a character count justified by a comment describing a system prompt it no longer matched. On its own that is merely crude. The cost came from what it composed with: `executeFullRetrievalPipeline` closes with a Lost-in-the-Middle reorder that appends even ranks and inserts odd ranks at the midpoint, so for 85 chunks the highest-scoring chunk lands at index 42, and `executeSearchStepWithChunks` renders only `chunks.prefix(10)`, which for that array is ranks 1, 3, 5 ... 19. The top match was therefore excluded before truncation even ran. The visible result was an inversion: on "How does dopamine affect social behavior?" Deep Think reported no evidence in the documents after 202.7s while Standard, whose 3 chunks fit its budget whole, answered correctly in 6.7s. Synthesis now packs from the chunks rather than the pre-rendered string, in score order with array position as a deterministic tiebreak, against a budget derived from `FoundationModelTokenBudget` and the real prompt, query and output reserve. The budgeted subset travels to the model as `sourceChunks` in the order it was labelled, because citations resolve positionally and reordering for relevance without carrying the order forward would have replaced a truncation defect with a misattribution one.
- **[Orchestration]** **The final synthesis step had two stacked cuts, and the smaller one did more damage.** `executeSynthesisStep` compressed each prior reasoning step with `prefix(150)` and then applied `prefix(3000)` to the join. The per-step cut ran first and truncates most steps mid-sentence regardless of how much room the overall budget actually had. Per-step allocation is now derived from the budget, divided across the steps present, and trimmed at a sentence boundary.
- **[Retrieval]** **Parent document expansion is gated on the token budget it feeds.** Expansion grew an 18-chunk result to 85 immediately before a synthesis stage that could hold roughly eight, so it was not a neutral addition: nothing it added fit, and the inflated array displaced the ranked set that did. Siblings are now admitted in parent-rank order and only while the projected evidence still fits the on-device window, sized with the on-device ratio regardless of where the query is allowed to run so the gate is not loosest exactly when the device is the destination. Primary matches are never gated, only siblings.
- **[Orchestration]** **Every evidence assembly now logs what it dropped.** Chunks kept, tokens used against budget, and chunks dropped, plus a warning when the highest-scoring chunk does not fit. Silent truncation is the defect class this pipeline keeps reproducing: four of the significant defects found this month share the shape where a stage discards data and every log line still reads healthy. A cut that cannot be seen in a trace is indistinguishable from no cut at all, which is how a run answering from 3.5% of its evidence looked identical to one answering from all of it.
- **[Ingestion]** **The embedding tokenizer padded its output, which made the app's token counter return a constant and silently discarded half of every document.** One `padding` block in two bundled `tokenizer.json` files caused three separate defects, and it had been shipping since the Core AI embedder landed.
  1. **`truncation.max_length: 128` against models exported at `[1, 512]`.** Both compiled models declare input shape `[1, 512]` (`scripts/compile_core_ai_model.py:50-51`) and both Swift providers already pad to 512, so the tokenizers were the only component capped at 128. Measured with the real WordPiece vocab over 139 live chunks: median chunk **273 tokens**, **125 of 139 (90%) truncated**, and **55% of all library content never reached the embedder**.
  2. **`padding.strategy.Fixed: 128` made `DocumentProcessor.countTokens` return 128 for every input, forever.** It returns `try tokenizer.encode(...).count`, and a padded encode is always the pad width. Proof, with no build required: **3,910 of 3,910** ingestion log lines across every recorded benchmark read exactly `maxTokens=128/430`. A real token count cannot be constant. The `safeTokenLimit` guard at 430 therefore never fired in production, and the chunker had no working measure of its own output.
  3. **Mean pooling averaged `[PAD]` vectors into every embedding.** `CoreMLSentenceEmbeddingProvider:464` builds `attentionMask = Array(repeating: 1, count: inputIds.count)` over *already padded* ids, so `padLength` is zero and the mask marks padding as real content. **Every vector ever computed by this app is diluted with padding.**
  Deleting the `padding` block fixes all three at once, because all three consumers already pad to their own fixed width themselves. Truncation is now 512, matching the models.
  **Two wrong turns are recorded so they are not repeated.** Raising `max_length` to 512 while *keeping* the padding block was tried first and was catastrophic: `countTokens` then returned 512, `512 > 430` fired for every chunk, and inside `splitOversizedChunkByTokens` every individual word also measured 512, so ingestion emitted roughly one chunk per word and case 1 exceeded a 1500s timeout having taken 250s before. That timeout was then misread as quadratic attention cost, which is also wrong: both models are fixed-shape `[1, 512]` and were always fed 512-wide tensors, so per-call inference cost does not change at all. It was a chunk explosion, not slow arithmetic.
  **Existing libraries do not benefit until re-embedded.** Old vectors remain valid 384-dimensional MiniLM embeddings of a truncated, padding-diluted input; they are not corrupt and will not crash. Nothing detects the change: `KnowledgeContainer` persists only `embeddingProviderId` and `embeddingDim`, neither of which moves. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: real WordPiece tokenization of 139 live chunks; 3910 constant log lines; BenchmarkRuns/tokfix showing varying counts of 386 to 430 after the fix]`
- **[UI]** **Library Settings told users the Core AI option was an Apple embedding model. It is the same MiniLM the other option runs.** `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md:3-8` binds the bundled `EmbeddingModel.mlpackage` to `sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2`, which is legally required attribution and the strongest provenance in this repository. The card read "Apple Intelligence-backed sentence embeddings", which a reader takes as an Apple-authored model. Both options are the same weights on two runtimes, so switching between them changes latency and not accuracy, and the copy now says exactly that. Two unmeasured figures went with it: "40%+ latency reduction" and "40%+ faster" both trace to `AppleIntelligenceTransitionPlan.md:16`, where a "40%+ reduction" is stated as a **goal**, with no measurement of it anywhere in the repo. `LIBRARY_SURFACES_AUDIT_2026-08-11.md:196` had already flagged them unverified and deferred them to a claim audit; this is that audit. "High semantic accuracy" on the MiniLM card was also an unmeasured superlative, and the first measurement contradicts it: over 25 QASPER cases the dense arm ranked the gold document first in 8% of cases against BM25's 60%. It was replaced with the verifiable property rather than deleted, because that figure is corpus-dependent and QASPER favours lexical matching. **Left alone deliberately:** the "25% match" figure on Smart Embedding Selection, whose derivation was not traced, and the same 40% claim in `USER_CHANGELOG.md:167`, which is a historical record of what was claimed at the time and should be corrected with a dated note rather than rewritten. `[evidence_level: code_verified + measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md:3-8, AppleIntelligenceTransitionPlan.md:16, BenchmarkRuns/cmp-standard]`
- **[Retrieval]** **Fusion discarded every keyword hit whenever the dense arm came back empty.** `reciprocalRankFusion` opened with `guard !vectorResults.isEmpty else { return [] }`, returning before the union logic below it, which already handles an empty vector arm correctly by appending every keyword-only candidate and falling back to BM25 order. Overnight measurement over 25 QASPER cases sets the stakes: the lexical arm ranks the gold document first in **60%** of cases against the dense arm's **8%**, so the set being thrown away was by far the stronger of the two. The guard now bails only when both arms are empty. **Separately and not fixed here: fusion is currently destructive even when both arms return results**, dropping r@1 from 0.600 lexical-alone to 0.360 after fusion, because the app weights the dense arm 0.60 against the lexical arm's 0.40. That is a weight decision and it needs a measured curve on more than one corpus before a default moves, so it is tracked as its own row rather than guessed at here. Note the offline `sweep_fusion_weight.py` cannot currently answer it: calibration fails at 47.2% order agreement against this run, most likely because the lexical tie-break changed in `d3226de`, and the script correctly refuses to print a curve it cannot reproduce. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: BenchmarkRuns/cmp-standard per-stage STAGE METRICS, 25 cases]`
- **[Retrieval]** **The app silently used two different ranking algorithms depending on a startup race.** `RAGEngine.init` loaded the Core ML cross-encoder in a fire-and-forget `Task` that nothing awaited, and `rerank` chose its path with `if let model = rerankerModel`. A query arriving before the load finished was ranked by the heuristic fallback; one arriving after was ranked by the cross-encoder. **Neither path announced itself**, so the heuristic was identifiable only by the absence of the cross-encoder log line, which is why it survived several benchmark runs unnoticed. Measured on 2026-08-16: two runs of one build over byte-identical documents produced `Re-ranking complete. Top score: 1.2705` in one and `AI Re-ranking: raw -11.52 to 5.61, normalized 0.10 to 0.90` in the other, and that is the upstream cause of the divergence recorded as the nondeterminism blocker. `rerank` now awaits the load, which cannot hang because a failed load still completes the task, and both paths log which one ran. **On device this decided whether a user's first query after launch was ranked like every later one.** After the fix the same paired comparison returns byte-identical answers, identical context length and an identical cross-encoder score range. A residual remains and is not claimed fixed: the keyword boost still counted 94 against 95 chunks across the pair, on an identical 90% hit rate, which did not reach the answer in this case but is not yet explained. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: BenchmarkRuns/rrfix-1 against rrfix-2, one paired case]`
- **[Retrieval]** **Suggested questions counted the filename as grounding, so they aligned with document titles rather than with what the documents say.** `isUsableLLMGeneratedQuestion` accepted a single overlap token from the document name, the section path, or the body, counted together. The filename term made the check vacuous: a question sharing nothing but words from the title passed as grounded. Only body and section tokens count now; section titles stay because they carry real content, filenames do not. Two further shapes are rejected at `violatesPassageQuestionGuardrails`, the chokepoint every live validator routes through. First, placeholder abstraction: "How does combining certain parts affect performance?" and "What characteristic might affect device reliability after sterilization?" are grammatical, on topic and unanswerable as posed, because the entity is hidden behind a stand-in the passage names outright. Second, trailing meta participles: a question ending in "described", "highlighted", "mentioned", "discussed" or "listed" asks what the text does rather than what is true. Matched only at the end, so "Which vessels are highlighted by the fluorescent dye?" survives. Verified against a surgical instructions-for-use document written to mirror the reported library: before, 5 questions of which 3 matched the reported failure shapes; after, 4 questions naming the sealing glass, the specific control units and the lens cement. **One remains wrong**, conflating a cleaning method with image latency, which the passage attributes to third-party control units instead. Question count falls as filters tighten, which is the intended trade.
- **[Infrastructure]** **Benchmark runs no longer write into the real library at all: the storage override is now pinned for the life of the process.** The harness set `--rag-validation-storage` and `WorkspaceSyncService.init` then ran `activateLocalWorkspace`, which calls `configureBaseDir(nil)` as ordinary workspace activation and silently destroyed the override, so every write landed in the owner's real library regardless of what storage the harness was given. That is the mechanism behind both the fixture pollution and the 2026-08-16 deletion. `OpenIntelligenceRuntimePaths.pinOverrides` now sets both directories and refuses every later mutation, printing each refusal. The pin also covers the cache directory, closing a second hole nobody had listed: `localCacheDirectory()` falls back to the real root rather than to the base override, so benchmark FTS5 writes reached the owner's real index on every run even while the base override was still alive, which is how that index came to hold 4,077 document ids for roughly 15 documents. No hard-boundary file was edited; `WorkspaceSyncService` is unchanged and its own activation logic still runs, it just can no longer clobber a pinned override. Proven with a live probe: real `ImportedDocuments` listing identical before and after, `documents_metadata.json` md5 identical, the real FTS5 database byte-identical with an untouched modification time, the sandbox holding both the ingested document and its own FTS5 index, and 4 refused clobber attempts in the log. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: pin-proof probe run 2026-08-16 against the ingesting build]`
- **[Infrastructure]** **The benchmark deleted six of the owner's documents, because it protected them with a hardcoded list that had gone stale.** `reset_shared_library` removed every file in the real `ImportedDocuments` whose name was not among four hardcoded entries last verified 2026-08-12. The library held fourteen, so the list under-protected by ten, and on 2026-08-16 a run destroyed six. Four more survived only because they happened to be in the benchmark's own fixture pool and were re-ingested moments later, which disguised the loss until a routine file count caught it. It also wrote `documents_metadata.json` to `[]`, which orphaned every real document and armed the app's own sweep to delete anything unreferenced and older than 900 seconds: the owner's files, never the fresh fixtures. The reset is now **additive-only**. It snapshots the library before the first case and removes only what the run itself added, which cannot go stale the way a hand-maintained list of names does, and it restores the original metadata rather than zeroing it. If the snapshot was never taken it **refuses to delete anything** rather than guessing. Verified two ways against a simulated fourteen-document library: all fourteen survive while three fixtures are removed and metadata is restored byte-identical, and a reset with no snapshot deletes nothing. The documents deleted on 2026-08-16 were restored from backup, now held at `~/Documents/OpenIntelligence-Library-Backups/`. **The pollution half is not fixed:** `WorkspaceSyncService.activateLocalWorkspace` still calls `configureBaseDir(nil)` and voids `--rag-validation-storage`, so runs still write into the real library. That file is a hard boundary and needs the owner to name it.
- **[Orchestration]** **CI broke because the iOS 27 error taxonomy does not exist in the SDK CI builds with.** `9d9fee6` through `5a2eecb` failed with `cannot find type 'LanguageModelError' in scope`. The runner installs Xcode 26.0.1 and 26.0 and selects the newest, while the types added for the error-taxonomy migration are iOS 27 only, so a change verified locally against Xcode 27 could not compile there. The failure was fast, 1m29s against 10 to 14 minutes for a healthy run, because it died in module emission rather than in the build. All four types are now behind `#if compiler(>=6.4)`, the same gate `FoundationModelRoutePolicy`, `FoundationModelTokenBudget` and `FoundationModelCapabilityProvider` already use for SDK-version-dependent Foundation Models API; on an older toolchain `mapModernError` returns nil and every caller falls through to its existing handling unchanged. A scan confirms zero remaining unguarded references. **Local verification cannot catch this class of break**, because the only Xcode 26 in play is on the runner.
- **[Retrieval]** **Retrieval ranking broke ties on a value that is regenerated every ingestion.** `byScoreThenId` resolved equal scores with `chunk.id.uuidString`, and its own comment states the intent: "The id tiebreak is arbitrary. The point is that it is the *same* arbitrary every run." A chunk id is a fresh UUID created at ingestion, so it was the same arbitrary only *within* one ingestion. Re-ingest the same file and every tie resolves differently, which also means two users holding the identical document rank it differently. The key is now `(sourceDocument, chunkIndex)`, stable across ingestions, devices and users, applied at all three retrieval sites; the remaining `uuidString` sort in `RAGService` builds a sync fingerprint rather than a ranking and is untouched. **This did not fix run-to-run nondeterminism and is not claimed to.** Two runs of the build carrying it still diverged: keyword hit rate 90% against 91% over byte-identical documents, 77 chunks filtered in one run and none in the other, MMR selecting 30 against 14. That hit rate is computed from chunk text over the candidate set, so a set differing in membership before any tie-break runs places the cause upstream, in embedding or vector search. An earlier test that appeared to confirm tie-breaking was confounded: holding ids fixed via a reused index also skipped re-embedding, controlling two variables while crediting one. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: BenchmarkRuns/tiefix-1 against tiefix-2]`
- **[Orchestration]** **Every Foundation Models error handler in the app was dead code on iOS 27.** The SDK deprecates all nine cases of `LanguageModelSession.GenerationError` and splits them across four new types, and the app caught only the old one, at three sites. So every error fell past its handler into a generic catch holding nothing but `localizedDescription`. A device capture on 2026-08-16 shows the cost: **17 of 17 failures were `GeneratedContent.ParsingError`**, each reported as "Session ended without producing a response" with zero streamed characters, and not one matched a typed catch. That is why the empty-response failure survived five disproved hypotheses, since the type carrying the evidence was never caught. Handling is now additive, so nothing that works today changes: `mapModernError` returns nil for anything that is not a new type and the old path runs unchanged. Three concrete gains beyond diagnosis: `GeneratedContent.ParsingError.rawContent` is now logged, which is the model output that failed to parse and has never been seen; `LanguageModelError.rateLimited` carries a real `resetDate` in place of the hardcoded `[2, 5, 12]` second backoff; and `contextSizeExceeded` reports Apple's own `tokenCount` and `contextSize` rather than the app's estimate. Separately, `ContentTaggingService` recovered by shortening text on context overflow and that branch had become unreachable on iOS 27, so every overflow was silently degrading to keyword tagging; it is restored through a shared `recoveryHint`, with a length check the original lacked that would have recursed forever on a text already under the limit.
- **[Retrieval]** **Suggested questions asked "why" about rules that documents state without ever justifying.** A lease says rent is late after the fifth day and never says why, so "Why does rent become late after the fifth day?" reads well, is clearly on topic, and has no answer anywhere in the document. That is the worst kind of suggestion, because the user only finds out after tapping it. `passageExplainsCause` now requires the passage to contain an actual explanation before a reason-seeking question survives, which is the same shape as the existing `how long` rule that requires an explicit duration. **The placement is the interesting part.** The service has five question validators and **two of them are dead code with zero call sites**, including `isAnswerableSuggestedQuestion`, where this guard was put first and therefore did nothing. A second copy in `isFaithfulQuestionRewrite` covered only the rewrite path. "Why can't the Tenant rent out the space?" survived both and reached the output. The rule now lives once in `violatesPassageQuestionGuardrails`, the single function every live path routes through, rather than being scattered across sites that can each be missed. Three causal markers were also dropped after being measured against the real lease: "the purpose" matched the boilerplate "for the purposes of this section", and "caused by" and "causes" matched "damage caused by the animal", which explains nothing. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: three headless probe runs against a residential lease; six of six questions grounded and zero reason-seeking questions in the final run, against two of six before]`
- **[Orchestration]** **Recursive research had no wall-clock bound, so a single query could run until the process was killed.** The loop was bounded by iterations and tokens, but each iteration runs a full retrieval pipeline plus a generation, which is unbounded work in practice. The citation guard exposed it rather than caused it: the one smoke case that reaches this loop previously escaped fast by taking a 59-character uncited answer, and once that shortcut was correctly refused the same case exceeded a 1500 second budget and returned nothing. The bad answer had been hiding the fact that this loop can spend arbitrarily long and still have nothing to show. A 180 second ceiling now ends the loop and lets the caller keep the reasoning chain's answer, which the caller always already holds. It is a hang stop rather than a tuning knob: healthy cases complete an entire query in 230 to 370 seconds, and only queries that retrieve beyond the 20-chunk fusion cap reach this loop at all.
- **[Orchestration]** **The citation guard was added to only one of the two call sites, and the one it missed is the one that reaches the user.** `executeRecursiveResearch` has two callers. The guard landed in `runVerificationLoop` (`maxIterations: 3`), while the low-confidence branch (`maxIterations: 5`) returns its result immediately, **before** the Deep Think verification loop, so nothing downstream inspects what it hands back. A 6-case smoke proved it rather than assumed it: the same case that exposed the defect still answered `"No evidence of a dataset of rumors in the provided context."` in 59 characters, and its log showed `Iteration 1/5` with no `[Self-RAG 2.0] Starting verification` line at all. Both sites are guarded now, and a grep confirms exactly two callers and exactly two places a recursive answer can become the final answer. The comment already sitting above that branch stated the rule the code was not enforcing: recursive research is an enhancement to an answer already in hand, and it cannot be allowed to take it away.
- **[Orchestration]** **The verification loop traded a grounded answer for an ungrounded one, and preferred it for exactly the reason it should have rejected it.** `AgenticPolicyService` retries when `groundingScore < 0.3 && totalCitations > 0`, while an answer carrying **no** citations has its grounding forced to 0.5 and skips the check entirely. So the uncited replacement always verified cleaner than the cited answer it displaced. The instrumentation added in this release caught the first case to hit the path and recorded both sides: `2647 chars with 4 citations -> 53 chars with 0 citations`, followed by a second pass replacing 53 chars with the same 53 chars because the semantic delta had collapsed. The guard is deliberately **not** a rule about length. An earlier reading of this defect assumed longer was better and was correctly rejected, because 12 of the 18 answers discarded in the 2026-08-15 run had themselves failed grounding. The rule is that a replacement may not take an answer's last citation away, since attributable answers are the product. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: BenchmarkRuns/smoke-deepthink-f6bb4ca case 85e41723, both sides logged]`
- **[Orchestration]** **Deep Think's fallback search looked up the literal word "query" in 83% of its invocations.** `getResearchDecision` instructed the model to `Reply with [ANSWER] (your answer) OR [SEARCH: query]`, and the on-device model copied the placeholder back verbatim. Measured over the 2026-08-15 run: **49 of 59 recursive searches searched for the string `query`**, across 16 of the 17 cases that reached the loop. Every one retrieved nothing relevant, the loop exhausted all its iterations, and `executeForcedSynthesis` then replaced the chain's cited answer with a stub. This is the mechanism behind the 49-to-195-character answers, and it is upstream of the grounding gate that was previously blamed for them. The prompt now names a slot instead of showing a word to copy, and `parseResearchDecision` rejects a placeholder echo rather than running a meaningless retrieval, because a wording change alone is not a guarantee on a small model. Recursive research is what fires when Deep Think is **least** confident, so this failed precisely on the questions that needed it most.
- **[Orchestration]** **Deep Think graded its own citations against the wrong array, and it has done so for as long as the reasoning chain has existed.** The chain labels sources `[S1]...[Sn]` over `routeChunksByTitle`'s output, which is a **reordering** of the caller's chunks, and it labels them **per rotating 4-chunk window**, restarting at `[S1]` every session. `runVerificationLoop` then resolved `[Sn]` against `allRetrievedChunks`, the pre-sort and pre-routing order. Two independent mismatches, so from session 2 onward nearly every citation was checked against an unrelated chunk. The measured consequence in the 2026-08-15 benchmark: the distinct cited source numbers across all 82 answers were S1 (51 cases), S2 (47), S3 (35), S4 (28), S5 (1), S6 (1), never spanning the 20-chunk array, because the model only ever saw four labels. `ReasoningChainResult` now carries `routedChunks` and verification resolves against it; the window passes `labelOffset: startIdx` so labels are global positions. `allRetrievedChunks -> sorted -> routed` were confirmed to be permutations of one set with nothing dropped, so this reorders the caller's sources rather than changing them. **Present verbatim in `v4.9.0`, so this is a long-standing defect being measured for the first time, not a 5.0 regression.**
- **[Orchestration]** **Every Deep Think and Maximum answer showed the user the same fabricated match score.** `RAGService` built the agentic `RAGAuditSnapshot` with `topSim: 0.7, secondSim: 0.5, avgTop5: 0.5` hardcoded. That value reaches the UI and is rendered as a percentage, so the quality figure beside every agentic answer was the constant 70% rather than a measurement. Confirmed downstream: `top_similarity` is exactly 0.7 on all 82 rows of the 2026-08-15 run, while the standard path varies between 0.80 and 1.27. Now computed from the retrieved chunks' actual similarity scores.
- **[Retrieval]** **Suggested questions were being written from bibliographies, tables of contents and running headers, because nothing ever judged the passage.** `SuggestedQuestionsService.buildGroundedPassages` took `chunks.prefix(limit)` with no quality test, so any chunk retrieval returned became question material. The service already carried five filters and every one of them inspects the **generated question**, which is why none of them caught this: a reference list is grammatical and dense with capitalised domain nouns, so it yields a perfectly well formed question such as "What is the role of Neurosci Yagishita Transient?" that no question-level test can distinguish from a real one. `questionSourcePenalty` now scores the source text on citation density, dot leaders, access boilerplate, digit-to-letter ratio, vowel-free token ratio and absence of sentence enders. It demotes rather than deletes, so a document that is entirely a reference list or a scanned table still produces suggestions instead of silently producing none. Verified against **5,425 real chunks from the local library**, not just hand-built samples, and the corpus run is what corrected the rule. The first version scored a parenthetical year as a bibliography signal and matched author initials loosely, which hard-demoted **51 chunks of ordinary body prose**: `(2015)` is the marker of an author-year citation *in running text*, so it fires hardest on the related-work paragraph, and `[A-Z][a-z]{2,}\s+[A-Z]{1,3}[,.]` matched "Detection DET." and "Twitter PHEME," as authors. The parenthetical-year signal is gone and author detection now requires three consecutive `Surname AB,` entries **with distinct surnames**, because "Phase A, Phase A, Phase B," is shape-identical to an author list and "Section A, Section B, Section C" is ordinary content in the manuals, forms and agreements this feature must not demote. Final state: **99.2% of real chunks score 0 and zero are falsely hard-demoted**, while bibliography, numbered references, table of contents, running header and numeric table all still demote hard. `[evidence_level: measured, confidence: high, evidence_source: the shipped function extracted verbatim and run over a 5,425-chunk stratified sample of LocalCache/FTS5/fulltext.sqlite, plus twelve hand-built samples; xcodebuild test 236 tests 0 failures]`
- **[Retrieval]** **The question-generation prompt only knew how to ask about research papers.** Its worked examples were "What is the primary methodology used to...", "What limitations did the study find in..." and "What is the main outcome of...", and it asked for "key concepts, methodologies, findings, and discussions". Every exemplar was paper-shaped, so a lease, a manual, a recipe or an invoice was pushed toward paper-shaped questions about a document that has no methodology and no findings. The prompt now names the document kind as the thing to match, with examples spanning a study, a manual, an agreement, a recipe, a report and a memo. Two em-dashes were also removed from prompt string literals: the model imitates the punctuation of its own instructions, so an em-dash in a prompt can reach the user inside a generated question. **Not yet verified on device against a spread of document types.**
- **[Retrieval]** **Every suggested question could arrive in the same sentence frame.** `enforceDiversity` spread questions across documents but never across phrasing, so a set of seven could open "What is the role of" four times, which reads as generated even when each question is individually fine. A cap of `max(2, count / 3)` per two-word frame now applies in both selection passes. A third pass ignores the cap, so a library that genuinely supports only one frame still returns the full requested count rather than a short list.
- **[Orchestration]** **Seven more sessions omitted `model:` and were missed by the grep that found the first ten.** The 2026-08-14 sweep searched for the no-argument `LanguageModelSession()`. These seven pass `instructions:` but still no model, so they never matched. An Instruments capture on 2026-08-15 caught two of them red-handed: `SourceOnlyAnswerService`'s extraction and review calls each returned **0 tokens over 6.5 and 7.1 seconds** with an empty `Response`, while all 30 other inferences in the same run succeeded. That service is the per-claim verifier that grades each claim as supported, contradicted, ambiguous or unsupported, so the strongest correctness check in the pipeline has been silently unavailable, returning nil and letting the answer through on its local checks alone. Also fixed: `ResponseTransformService` (two sites), `WritingToolsService`, `DocumentSummaryService` and `ContentTaggingService`. A structural scan now confirms **zero** remaining `LanguageModelSession` constructions without a model.
- **[UI]** **Every citation tap in a grounded answer was a no-op.** `GroundedAnswerView` turns `[3]` into a tappable link with a regular-expression replacement whose template was `"[[$$1]](citation://$$1)"`. In an ICU replacement template the escape for a literal dollar is `\$`, not `$$`, so `$$1` emits a literal `$` followed by capture group 1. Citations became `citation://$3`, which LaunchServices cannot resolve. A device log from 2026-08-15 records it as `Failed to open URL citation://$3` with `LSApplicationWorkspaceErrorDomain Code=115`. The `OpenURLAction` handler below it was correct all along and simply never received a URL it could parse, because `Int("$3")` is nil. Template is now `$1`.
- **[UI]** **Suggested questions degraded into fragments, caused by the session-instruction fix that shipped hours earlier.** `SuggestedQuestionsService` was one of ten services converted from the bare `LanguageModelSession()` initialiser, and each conversion added a short instruction. The instruction given to this one ended in "Be concise", while both of its prompts demand the opposite: complete grammatical sentences, one explicitly warning "Never output trailing fragments or cut-off phrases". Session instructions outrank prompt text, so the model was told to be terse by the instruction and told not to produce fragments by the prompt, and produced fragments. It is also the **only** one of the ten that uses schema-constrained generation, `respond(to:generating:)` against a `@Generable` type, where a competing directive does the most damage; the other eight generate a label, a query or a short summary, and their instructions agree with their prompts. The instruction now states the role and defers formatting to the request. Worth noting how it surfaced: these two call sites were failing before the conversion, so the app had been serving its fallback questions, and fixing the call exposed a worse LLM path than the fallback it replaced.
- **[Orchestration]** **The tokens generated before an empty response were being held in memory and discarded unread.** An Instruments capture on 2026-08-14 shows these requests are not empty at the framework level: Apple records `Error Count: 0`, a `generatedTokenCount` of 21 to 49, and `deltasCount: 2`, while the final `Response` in `ModelInferenceTable` is the empty string. Tokens are emitted and then resolve to nothing. `responseText` accumulates `snapshot.content` from the stream and is still in scope in the catch, so those tokens were always available and never logged. The generic catch now records their length and first 400 characters. Diagnostics only. This matters because it makes the remaining unknown answerable from an ordinary device log rather than from Instruments, and the last instrumented run produced **10 memory warnings and 4 drops into efficient mode** where a comparable un-instrumented run produced none, so heavy profiling measures a differently behaving app.
- **[Orchestration]** **The recursive-research guard was added to the wrong call site.** `executeRecursiveResearch` has two callers. The one guarded earlier on 2026-08-14 passes `maxIterations: 5` and sits in the reasoning-chain fallback; the one that actually fires in Deep Think is the Self-RAG verification retry and passes `maxIterations: 3`. Device logs identify which ran unambiguously, because the iteration count is printed: `[RecursiveResearch] Iteration 1/3`. Guarding one and not the other left the failure completely intact while appearing fixed, and produced three rebuild cycles in which the containment log line never appeared and the query kept dying. The Self-RAG site was a bare `try await`, so a throw skipped the handling immediately below it, which already keeps the previous answer when research returns an *empty* one. Both sites are now guarded.
- **[Orchestration]** **Ten services built their language model session with the bare `LanguageModelSession()` initialiser, and every one of them failed deterministically.** The initialiser supplies no model and no instructions. Every path that works goes through `FoundationModelSessionFactory`, which builds `LanguageModelSession(model: SystemLanguageModel.default, tools:, instructions:)`. The ones that did not, failed with `ParsingError` / "Session ended without producing a response", which `localizedDescription` renders as the misleading "Failed to parse generated content". The decisive experiment was an **empty library** with the one-word query **"Test"** and zero retrieved chunks: the main answer generated normally and `SmartReplyService` still failed, which rules out content, safety guardrails, context size and output token caps in a single run. An Instruments capture of the Foundation Models template shows `assets: ""` on exactly these responses, consistent with a session that never received its model assets, against a full asset list on every response that succeeded. This was never new. `[SmartReply] LLM follow-up generation failed` appears in captures from 2026-07-30 on v4.7 and 2026-08-03 on v4.9, and `SmartReplyService fails to parse generated content on every run` has been an open item in `Docs/ai/STATE.md` the whole time. It stayed invisible because it only cost a follow-up suggestion chip. `ImageUnderstandingService` failed five times in one capture for the same reason. Fixed at all ten sites: `PromptEvaluationService`, `ConversationMemoryService`, `ImageUnderstandingService`, `IterativeRetrievalService`, `LibraryVisualizationEngine`, `ClusterLabelService`, `SuggestedQuestionsService` (twice), `SmartReplyService` and `QueryRewriterService`.
- **[Orchestration]** **"Session ended without producing a response" was the output token cap being hit, and Apple's error said nothing about it.** An Instruments capture using the Foundation Models template on device (iPhone 16 Pro Max, iOS 27.0) recorded the failing request directly: `maximumResponseTokens: 64`, `generatedTokenCount: 64`, `deltasCount: 2`, `assets: ""`, and a delta transcript of `{"entries":[]}`. The model runs into the cap, the transcript delta comes back empty, and the SDK reports `ParsingError` / "Failed to parse generated content", which names the symptom and hides the cause entirely. Truncation here does not degrade a response, it erases it. Every generation that succeeded in the same trace finished well under its cap: 107, 111, 85 and 321 generated tokens against caps of 700 and 1500. The title-routing turn asked for a comma-separated list in 64 tokens, which is ample for the reply and not for whatever the model emits on the way to it; it is now 256. The recursive-research decision prompt asks for `[ANSWER] (your answer)` in 600 and is now 900, matching the headroom the reasoning chain runs with. Four hypotheses were investigated and ruled out before the trace made this visible: rate limiting (disproved by an escalating 2s/5s/12s backoff failing identically), guided generation against a `@Generable` schema (disproved by a plain prose call failing the same way), prompt size (disproved by 2903-token prompts succeeding while 1435-token prompts failed), and empty context (disproved because the reasoning chain passes `context: ""` and succeeds). Input size was never the variable. The output cap was.
- **[Orchestration]** **A failure in recursive research destroyed the answer the reasoning chain had already produced.** The call was `try await` with no `catch`, so any throw inside it propagated out and failed the entire query. The block immediately below it already handled "research found nothing better" by falling through to the chain's result; only a *throw* skipped that path. A device capture on 2026-08-14 shows the exact sequence: the chain generated eight session insights and a final synthesis successfully, then recursive research returned an empty response four times, and the user received a failed query rather than the answer that was sitting in hand. Recursive research is an enhancement to an answer already obtained and must not be able to take it away. This became urgent because of a change made earlier the same day: the gate that decides whether to research further now fires on measured evidence coverage rather than on a prose heuristic, so this path runs far more often than it used to, which turned a latent failure into a constant one. Correlating every generation in that capture against its outcome isolated it precisely: maxTokens 700 succeeded eight times and 1500 succeeded once, all inside the reasoning chain, while 64 (the title-routing turn) and 600 (recursive research) returned empty every time.
- **[Orchestration]** **The one log line that would explain "Failed to parse generated content" sat behind a typed catch the error never matched.** `AppleFoundationLLMService.generate` logs the full SDK error before mapping, because the mapper collapses Apple's case and its `Context`, where the actual reason lives, into a generic string. That log is inside `catch let error as LanguageModelSession.GenerationError`, and the failure hitting this app is a **`ParsingError`**, so it escaped with only its `localizedDescription` intact and the diagnostic never ran. The function already carries a comment recording the same class of miss for cancellation thrown out of `for try await`. A generic `catch` now logs the error's concrete type, its full `String(describing:)`, its description and the token estimate, then rethrows unchanged. Diagnostics only. This matters because two hypotheses were acted on earlier the same day and both were wrong: an escalating backoff was added on the theory that this was Apple Intelligence throttling, and it disproved itself by failing identically at 2s, 5s and 12s; then a prose fallback was added on the theory that guided generation could not satisfy the `@Generable` schema, and a device capture then showed a **plain 64 token prose request with `context: nil` failing with the same `ParsingError`**, which rules that out too. Both changes are worth keeping on their own merits, and neither was diagnosis. The next capture will carry Apple's actual reason.
- **[Orchestration]** **A schema failure lost the whole query, and every remedy in the pipeline assumed it was transient.** The final answer is produced with Apple Foundation Models guided generation, `session.respond(to:generating:)` against a `@Generable` schema, and "Failed to parse generated content" is what that throws when the model cannot produce conforming output. There was no fallback: the error propagated, the query failed, and the user got nothing. A device capture on 2026-08-14 shows how badly the existing recovery machinery misreads this. In one run **fifteen reasoning sessions generated successfully in prose and all six final syntheses failed**, deterministically. Before giving up the pipeline reduced the prompt twice (2927, then 2491, then 1565 estimated tokens) and the new escalating backoff retried the same constrained call three more times across 19 seconds. Shrinking assumes a size problem; backing off assumes throttling. It was neither, which the backoff proved by failing identically at every step. A schema failure now degrades to prose instead of terminating. This is a real loss and is labelled as such: no per-claim citations, no model confidence, no matched terms, and `structuredRAGGeneration` is nil so downstream treats the answer as unstructured. Inline `[Sn]` markers are recovered from the text so citations the model did write still resolve, and the response is tagged `(Prose fallback)` so it is never mistaken for a structured answer. If prose fails too, the original schema error is rethrown, because it is the more informative one.
- **[Orchestration]** **The title-routing turn failed on device and reported only that it had failed.** The first device run after it shipped logged `Title routing unavailable; keeping retrieval order` and nothing else, because the call used `try?` and swallowed the error. The fallback behaved correctly and the chain proceeded on retrieval order with no harm, which is the whole point of building it to reorder rather than drop; but a fallback that cannot say why it fell back never gets fixed, and a second capture would have learned nothing. It now catches the error, logs its type and description, and separately logs the model's actual reply when the parser finds no usable number, since the parser accepts digits anywhere and reaching that branch means the wording is the problem.
- **[Retrieval]** **The citation map only fired on the standard route, so the mode most likely to produce a cited answer could not audit its citations.** It was added inline in the standard query path; the first device capture afterwards contained **zero** citation maps, because Deep Think does not take that path. Rather than write a second copy for the agentic route, which is precisely how five call sites came to independently assume `prefix(used)`, the map is now one shared `logCitationMap` helper called from both, tagged with the route it ran on.
- **[Retrieval]** **The fusion weight was being tuned blind, on a signal that inverts between libraries.** Two numbers were missing from the device log and both are needed to reason about hybrid retrieval at all. `fts5OnlyHits` existed with no counterpart, so a capture told you what the lexical arm uniquely found and never what the dense arm uniquely found. And the effective weight, which varies per query (container preset, plus the intent delta from `SearchIntent.weightAdjustment`, then the [0.35, 0.65] clamp), was logged at `.debug` on the way into the search and appeared **zero times** in a 333KB device capture. The completion line now reports `unique: N vector-only, M lexical-only` alongside the weights actually used, at `.info`. Why this matters more than the number itself: captures from 2026-08-14 show the arms inverting between real libraries. A manuals corpus produced 120 vector + 60 FTS5 with 57 lexical-only, while a research corpus asked about "the role of serotonin signaling" produced **140 vector + 6 FTS5**, because "serotonin" appears in 51% of its chunks and discriminates nothing. The offline sweep's result that dense weight 0.00 maximises MRR@10 was measured on `qasper_external_v1`, whose questions were written by readers shown only a title and abstract and therefore reuse the paper's vocabulary. That is a fixture property. Setting the weight to zero globally would have been right for QASPER and ruinous for the serotonin corpus.
- **[Retrieval]** **Nothing reported which document each citation label pointed at.** That is why labels could resolve to the wrong document for an unknown length of time while every guard passed: indices were in range, chunks were real, only the mapping was wrong. `RAGService` now logs a citation map (`S1=file p.20 Section | S2=…`) when the prompt's sources are finalised, noting how many arrived via needle rescue. A cited answer can now be checked against its sources by reading one log line.
- **[Orchestration]** **The reasoning chain chose what to read by cosine similarity, before anything had been read.** Session windows were carved out of the retrieval ranking, so if the reranker put the wrong chunks on top, every session reasoned over them and the answer chunk was never opened. The chain now opens with a routing turn: `routeChunksByTitle` builds a menu from `chunk.metadata.sectionTitle`, one line per candidate, and asks the model which sections would answer the question. This is only affordable because of the size asymmetry it exploits. A section title costs about fifty characters and a chunk of body text several hundred, so twenty titles fit inside one 3500 character session window where three chunks do not, and one 64 token generation buys an informed ordering for every session that follows. It **reorders and never drops**: unselected candidates move behind the selected ones, every failure path returns the input untouched, and the menu is capped at 1800 characters so it cannot overrun the window it exists to save. The worst case is therefore the retrieval ordering the chain previously received unconditionally, plus one generation. Chunks without a structured section fall back to their opening text, which is the case the routing turn is most useful for. Note the irony this addresses: a device trace on 2026-08-14 shows the model handed a document's actual table of contents as an evidence chunk, where it is worthless. As a routing menu it is the most valuable thing in the library.
- **[Orchestration]** **A reasoning session spent about 22% of its window on evidence it had not already seen.** Against Apple FM's 4096 token context (`FoundationModelTokenBudget.baseContextLength`, ~1.4 chars per token), a Deep Think session gets 3500 characters. Two things consumed most of it. Session windows used `stride = chunksPerSession / 2`, a deliberate 50% overlap "for continuity", which spends ~1750 characters per session re-reading text the previous session already read: across eight sessions that is roughly four entire session budgets returned to material already seen. With 20 chunks at 4 per session, half-overlap covers 18 of them in eight sessions and reads each twice, while disjoint windows cover all 20 in five. And `PRIOR FINDINGS` carries up to 1200 characters of free-text recap, which the file's own budget comment confirms ("Session 2+: 2200 context + 1200 insights + 300 prompt"). Windows are disjoint now, and each session prompt additionally carries one compact line naming what the chain has still not answered, drawn from the `FactBank` decomposition that already existed: about eighty characters, and it tells a session what to hunt rather than what was found. Prose degrades and the line cannot: device captures show `PRIOR FINDINGS` decaying into "[S1] contains a section titled" and twice into the session's own system prompt. Continuity is meant to come from carried state, not from re-reading raw text.
- **[Retrieval]** **The model was shown citation labels but never the citation range.** A device trace on 2026-08-14 shows an answer citing `[S5]` and `[S6]` against a four source prompt. Nothing in the prompt ever stated how many sources existed, so a source the model inferred was indistinguishable to it from one it had read. `assembleContext` now ends with `Cite only sources S1 to Sn`. Semantic labels such as `[NIR-FI-Laparoscopes §4.8.1]` would close the same gap and cost roughly two hundred characters across eight sources, which is about 5% of a Deep Think session's entire evidence budget; at a 4096 token context the thirty character version wins. This is a guard rail rather than the fix: the ordering and namespace defects fixed alongside it are what made the citations wrong.
- **[Retrieval]** **Citations resolved to the wrong document on any query with four or more chunks.** `RAGEngine.assembleContext` applies Lost-in-the-Middle reordering before it numbers sources, emitting `front + back.reversed()` so that four chunks `[A,B,C,D]` are shown to the model as `[A,C,D,B]` under labels `[S1]` through `[S4]`. Every caller then rebuilt the citation list as `prefix(used)` of its own **pre-reordering** array. So a model citing `[S2]` meant C while the response resolved it to B: the first position matched by coincidence and the rest were wrong. Nothing downstream could detect it, because every index was in range and every citation resolved to a real chunk, just not the one the model had read. Five call sites made the same mistake independently (`orderedCandidates.prefix(actualChunksUsed)`, two copies of `orderedCandidates.prefix(localAssembly.used)`, and `trimmed.prefix(used)`), which is what happens when the label and the chunk are related by convention rather than by construction. `assembleContext` now returns `sources`, the chunks in the exact order their labels were assigned, and every caller uses it. The invariant is now structural: the array the prompt labelled and the array the response attaches are the same array.
- **[Orchestration]** **The decision to research further was made by reading the answer's prose, while the measurement that answers the same question was computed and thrown away.** In Deep Think and Maximum, `executeRecursiveResearch` is the only genuine agentic loop in the app: it asks the model to decide, parses `.answer` or `.search(query)`, and re-retrieves on demand. Its single call site was gated on `answerIndicatesRetrievalMiss(chainResult.finalAnswer)`, a string heuristic scanning for hedging like "do not specify". That fails in both directions and both were observed on device. A confidently wrong answer contains no hedging, so the loop never ran when it was most needed; and a grounded synthesis that correctly notes a gap does contain hedging, so a 91% confidence answer was discarded in favour of a raw evidence dump, which is why a second guard exists below that call. Meanwhile `FactBank` had already decomposed the query into sub-questions, tracked which ones the evidence answered, and compared the result against the coverage target the chain uses for its own early-stop decision. None of it reached the decision. `ReasoningChainResult` now carries `evidenceCoverage`, `evidenceCoverageTarget` and `unansweredQuestions`, and the gate fires when the chain finished below its own target, which is a measurement rather than an inference about wording and introduces no new threshold. The prose heuristic is kept as an additional trigger rather than replaced, because it catches a case coverage cannot see: retrieval that confidently returned the wrong subject entirely, where every sub-question reads as answered.
