And the research is now aimed at the unanswered sub-questions rather than re-running the original query, since re-running it searches for what was already found.
- **[Retrieval]** **One prompt carried two independent citation numbering schemes, so an answer cited sources that did not exist.** A device trace on 2026-08-14 shows the response "[S6] and [S5] state that the main approach for sterilization involves..." against **four** retrieved chunks. `assembleContext` packs chunks until the character budget runs out and labels them `[S1]`...`[S{used}]`. Needle Rescue then takes the chunks that did **not** fit and runs sentence extraction over them, appending the result to the same prompt, and `extractRelevantSentences` labelled its sources `[S1]`... over its own array, re-indexed from zero. The model was therefore shown four sources followed by two more sources also numbered one and two, and cited the last two as the fifth and sixth it had seen. The pipeline detected the problem three times and none of it reached the reader: `StructuredAnswer.citedEvidence` drops out-of-range citations, the claim then hits the empty-evidence confidence floor of 0.15, `normalizeStructuredCitations` filters anything outside `1...maxSourceCount`, and Gate B (Evidence Coverage) failed as designed. The one thing not validated was the answer prose, which is the only part the user reads. Rescue now continues the packed block's numbering through a `labelOffset`, and the chunks it contributed are attached to the response, because a prompt that hands a source a citation label must be able to resolve that label afterwards. `SentenceExtractionResult` gained `usedSourceIndices` to make that possible; returning only a count is what allowed the two halves to disagree.
- **[Orchestration]** **A rate-limited generation retried once after two seconds with an identical prompt, so it failed identically and the query was lost.** Deep Think fires many generations for a single question: a device capture on 2026-08-13 recorded **16 generations and four rate limits** for one query. The last generation is the answer, so the user waited through eight reasoning sessions and then lost the query with all of its evidence already gathered. Two seconds does not clear an Apple Intelligence throttle that has not expired, and resending the same prompt at the same temperature changes nothing about why it was refused. `RAGService` now backs off on an escalating ladder of 2, 5 and 12 seconds, logging each attempt and the cumulative wait. Two details matter and both come from the capture. The ladder does **not** abort when a retry fails with something other than a rate-limit error: the observed retry failure was a `ParsingError` carrying "Session ended without producing a response", which is what a still-throttled on-device session looks like from the outside rather than a malformed prompt, so treating it as permanent is the bug being fixed. And cancellation is now checked explicitly after each sleep, because the existing `try?` swallows it; across a 19 second ladder a cancelled query would otherwise keep issuing generations after the user had moved on. Worst case adds 19 seconds to a query that already spent minutes in the reasoning chain.
- **[Retrieval]** **Deep Think reasoned over section headings instead of document text, and the query failed.** Found in a device log from a physical iPhone, which is the first on-device evidence this project has had. For "How often must reprocessing occur?" retrieval worked: 88.5ms, 177 candidates, 57 chunks found by FTS5 and missed by vector search, reranker normalising the top chunk from 0.10 to 0.90 with the correct section first. `RAGService.extractRelevantSentences` then handed the reasoning chain **67 characters against a 3000+ character budget**, consisting of a section label and a filename. Eight Deep Think sessions ran on that, insights degraded to "[S1] contains a section titled", and the final generation failed to parse. Two independent causes, both fixed. Selection required a query keyword as a raw substring with no stemming, while FTS5 indexes with `porter unicode61`, so retrieval matched "reprocessed" to a query saying "reprocessing" and selection then discarded exactly those sentences, leaving only lines with the literal token. On a numbered manual the only such line is the heading. Separately a line counted as a heading only if it contained no digits anywhere, which rejects every heading in a technical document ("4 Reprocessing", "4.5.1 Overview"), so nothing beneath a section could inherit its keywords. Keyword matching is now additive over raw substring and a shared suffix-stripped stem, so every line that scored before still scores; the digit test applies after a leading section number is stripped; and the section is seeded from `chunk.metadata.sectionTitle`, the same field FTS5 already ranks at a bm25 weight of 10.0, rather than being re-derived from raw text by a heuristic. None of the seven per-stage retrieval metrics could see any of this, because sentence selection runs downstream of the last measured stage.
- **[Orchestration]** **A degenerate reasoning context was not treated as a missing one.** `AgenticOrchestrator` falls back to raw chunk content when sentence extraction returns nothing, but the test was `isEmpty`, and a 67-character section title is not empty, so the fallback never fired through eight sessions. It now requires a context large enough to reason over.
- **[Orchestration]** **The model's own instructions were recorded as findings and shown to the user.** Two Deep Think sessions logged "You are a document analyst. Describe what these documents add about:" as their insight. That text is the session's own system prompt. `stripPromptEchoAndToolNoise` already existed for this class of contamination and missed it, because its rules hunt JSON envelopes, tool-call blobs and markup, and this returned as ordinary prose. The echo also reached the user directly, since `insight` is the `output` of the emitted `ThinkingStep`, and it was condensed into the next session's prior findings. The stripper now takes the session's actual system prompt and drops a leading sentence whose meaningful words overlap it by 70% or more, bounded to two short leading sentences so a genuine finding reusing the prompt's vocabulary cannot be eaten. Matching is by token overlap rather than literal prefix because what came back was a paraphrase: the prompt says "add beyond the prior findings" and the echo said "add about".
- **[General]** **The benchmark's audit trail was blank for the five stages where every retrieval decision is made.** `DebugRAGValidationHarness` emits `STAGE METRICS` beside `STAGE SOURCES` so that, in the words of its own doc comment, "any number in the first block can be recomputed by hand from the second. A benchmark that cannot be checked is an assertion, not a measurement." `STAGE SOURCES` printed `chunk.sourceDocument`, a display name, and `sourceDocument` is attached by `RAGService` after hybrid search returns. So `vector`, `lexical`, `fusion`, `boosted` and `candidates` every one recorded `(unnamed)`, and the only two stages that could be checked by hand were `rerank` and `final`, which nobody was questioning. The metrics themselves were never wrong: `groundTruth` is resolved to document ids before `RetrievalStageEvaluator.score` sees it, a few lines above in the same function. This was a hole in the evidence, not in the numbers, and it meant the finding that RRF fusion scores worse than the lexical arm alone (26 wins to 6, exact sign test p=0.0005) could not be independently verified from any artifact the project had kept. Each entry is now `<chunkId>#<documentId>#<name>`. Both ids are required and answer different questions: `RAGEngine.reciprocalRankFusion` keys on `chunk.id`, so replaying a fusion needs chunk identity, while relevance is judged per document. The per-stage record also deepened from 20 candidates to 100, because 20 is sufficient to re-derive a metric whose deepest cutoff is 10 and insufficient to re-derive a *different* fusion weight, where reweighting can lift an item from below the old cap into the top ten.
- **[General]** **A paused benchmark run recorded the weight it was testing nowhere but its directory name.** `results.json` carries the run parameters but is written only after the whole loop completes, so the run interrupted at 36 of 83 cases on 2026-08-13 preserved its `--vector-weight` only in the string `qasper-vw30`, typed by hand. `run_quality_matrix.py` now writes `run_config.json` when the run starts. On resume the recorded file wins and a conflicting argument is refused rather than merged, because resuming with a different weight would append cases scored under a second configuration into one `results.jsonl` with nothing downstream able to tell the halves apart.
- **[General]** **An out-of-tree manifest crashed the run at the last step, after all the compute.** `str(manifest_path.relative_to(REPO_ROOT))` raises rather than falling back, and it sat in the `results.json` write that closes a multi-hour job. Fixture packs are legitimately built into scratch directories. Both call sites now go through `repo_relative()`.
- **[General]** **The headless validation harness killed its own run on iOS.** `runIfNeeded` returned the string `"No configuration or already running."` when another caller already held the run gate, and `runHeadlessIfNeeded` could not distinguish that from a finished report, so it printed the sentence and called `exit(0)`. Two paths race for the gate: `App.init` and `ContentView`. On macOS the headless path usually claims it first and the bug stays hidden; in the Simulator `ContentView` claims first, so the process terminated a few seconds in with `ValidationOutput/` created and no report ever written, which reads exactly like a hung run. `runIfNeeded` now returns `String?` and yields `nil` to the loser, only the winner prints and exits, and `ContentView` exits after its own successful run so a `simctl launch` caller is not left waiting forever. Whole harness is inside `#if DEBUG`.
- **[Orchestration]** **Two exact-match agentic tools were declared and never registered, and the benchmark says they are the ones worth having.** `FoundationModelToolRegistry.createTools` is the only place tools reach a `LanguageModelSession`, and it appended four of the twelve `Tool` structs in that file. `count_pattern` and `search_exact_pattern` are now registered, taking the live set to six. The other six stay unregistered on purpose: each duplicates a tool already there (`search_documents` and `find_related_documents` by `retrieve_corpus_evidence`, `list_documents` and `get_corpus_stats` by `get_library_overview`, `get_document_summary` by `inspect_document`, `compare_documents` by `compare_topic_across_documents`), and handing the model six near-duplicates spends context budget every turn and degrades tool selection. **Why these two:** on the 2026-08-12 external benchmark the lexical arm scored MRR@10 **0.65** against the dense arm's **0.28**, so exact matching is what this pipeline is currently best at and nothing registered exposed it to the model directly; extractive fact-lookup was the weakest answer category at **19/51 correct**, which is exactly the question these serve. `Step 4.8 Targeted Spec Retrieval` already exists in the pipeline for the same reason, its recorded rationale being a measured cross-encoder prose bias of roughly 0.78 for prose against 0.30 for tables. **This is a hypothesis, not a finding**: re-run `qasper_external_v1` and compare the `exact_value` category before concluding it helped. A claim audit run first established that the six unregistered wrappers are **not** dead code as the roadmap row asserted; the `RAGService` methods behind them are live and under test in `CoreValidationView` and `ContainerScopingSelfTestsView`, so nothing was deleted.
- **[Retrieval]** **RRF fusion scored worse than the lexical arm alone, and the weight clamp is why.** `QueryProfile.adjustedHybridWeights` clamps both arms to `max(0.35, min(0.65, ...))`, guaranteeing the dense arm at least 35% of the fusion however badly it performs. That is a sound default when both arms are comparable, which is what the RRF literature (Cormack et al. 2009, already cited in `RESEARCH_PAPERS_REFERENCE_SHEET.md`) assumes. The 2026-08-12 benchmark showed they are not comparable on this content: `vector` MRR@10 **0.28** against `lexical` **0.65**, and fusion measurably *worse* than lexical alone, 26 wins to 6 over 72 paired cases, exact two-sided sign test **p = 0.0005**. The floor is what prevents the adaptive policy from correcting for it. **No product default has been changed**; changing the clamp is a product decision and wants a measurement first. `--rag-validation-vector-weight` and `run_quality_matrix.py --vector-weight` set the dense-arm weight for a benchmark run, bypassing the clamp because the clamp is the hypothesis under test, and the value is printed in each report as `Fusion Weights:`. The override lives behind `#if DEBUG` and is read from a UserDefaults key nothing else writes, so a normal launch and the test suite are untouched. First smoke case at 0.2/0.8: `lexical` MRR 1.00, `vector` 0.00, and `fusion` 1.00 instead of being dragged down.
- **[General]** **The benchmark could not sweep retrieval breadth, which is where its largest measured loss is.** `DebugRAGValidationHarness` called `queryWithAudit` without a `topK`, taking its default of 3. That default is correct and deliberate: the chat UI uses the same value via `@AppStorage("retrievalTopK")`, so an unflagged run measures shipped behaviour rather than a benchmark-only setting. It also meant the one variable most implicated in the 2026-08-12 results could not be varied. On that run the right document reached the **final ranking on 75% of missed cases**: the answer was arriving in the candidate set and being truncated before it reached the model, with `final` carrying a mean of 8 chunks against `rerank`'s 90. `--rag-validation-topk` now sets it, `run_quality_matrix.py --top-k` passes it, and the value is printed in each run's report as `Retrieval TopK:` so an archived artifact records what produced it. Omitting the flag changes nothing. Whole harness is inside `#if DEBUG`.
- **[General]** **The benchmark harness scored 0.0000 at every stage whenever it reused an index, while retrieval was in fact working.** `DebugRAGValidationHarness.stageMetricsLines` resolved an expected filename to a document id by scanning retrieved chunks for a matching `sourceDocument`. That field is only populated for documents ingested in the same launch, so under `--rag-validation-skip-ingest` every chunk carried an empty name, nothing resolved, and every metric read zero: a query against a reused index returned **42 candidates at `rerank` and reported `R@5 0.00`**. Resolution now prefers the service's loaded document metadata, which is present whether or not this launch did the ingesting, and falls back to the old chunk scan. First writer wins per filename stem, so a later `-N` duplicate of the same source cannot displace the original and re-ingesting a file cannot change scoring. This is what makes a reused index scorable, and therefore the difference between re-ingesting the distractor pool for all 83 cases and ingesting it once: roughly four hours against roughly fifteen minutes. The entire file sits inside `#if DEBUG`, so none of it reaches a shipping build. Builds; **not yet verified end to end**, because the verification run was interrupted.
- **[General]** **The benchmark harness reported the same statistical power at every sample size.** `minimum_detectable_effect(n)` in `scripts/run_quality_matrix.py` interpolated `n` into its sentence but the threshold it quoted was a hardcoded constant, so it printed "differences below about 25 points are not resolvable" at n=18 and would have printed it unchanged at n=1000. Under the exact two-sided sign test the function itself describes, `2 * 0.5**d < 0.05` first holds at **d=6**, and that part was right and is genuinely independent of `n`; the smallest *difference* that can reach it is b=6, c=0, which is `6/n`. At n=18 that is **33 points, not 25**, so the harness had been overstating its own sensitivity by a third, in the direction that makes a flat run look more meaningful than it was. The figure had propagated into `Docs/ai/RUNBOOK.md`, `Docs/ai/STATE.md` and the Notion row tracking this work, and every report under `BenchmarkRuns/` dated before 2026-08-12 carries it; those power statements should be read as `6/n`. The function now computes the value and says so, and reports a plain refusal when `n` is below 6 rather than quoting a threshold that cannot be reached. This is the third scoring or reporting defect found in this harness, after the seven metric bugs of 2026-08-08 and the single-source ground-truth bug of 2026-08-11.
- **[General]** **`prepare_rag_research_fixtures.py --download` would pull non-commercial dataset content into a public repository.** FinanceBench is CC BY-NC 4.0 and DocVQA redistribution is not granted by its portal terms, yet both were reachable as an ordinary side effect of a flag whose name says nothing about licensing. Both now refuse unless `--accept-non-commercial` is passed for an explicitly local run. Recorded alongside it: `allenai/scifact` is CC BY-NC 2.0 while `BeIR/scifact` advertises CC BY-SA 4.0 for substantially the same data, so that dataset is avoided rather than resolved in the convenient direction.
- **[Orchestration]** **The planner would attempt Private Cloud Compute on a quota state its own route gate counts as unauthorized.** `RouteEvalMetrics.RouteInvariant.quotaFailClosed` states the contract in its doc comment: "`.limitReached`, `.unsupported`, and `.unknown` are all fail-closed states. None of them may be accompanied by a PCC attempt." The planner never implemented it. `FoundationModelCapabilitySnapshot.canUsePCC` tested `pccQuota != .limitReached` alone, so `.unknown` authorised a cloud attempt that the scorer would have recorded as an `unauthorizedCloudAttempts` violation. **This is reachable in production, not only in tests:** `FoundationModelCapabilityProvider` maps `pcc.quotaUsage.status` through an `@unknown default`, so any status a future SDK adds becomes `.unknown` while `hasPCCEntitlement` and `pccAvailable` are both true. The planner was fail-open on exactly the state the gate exists to catch, and the first real route-gate run would have reported it and been right. The rule now lives once, as `PCCQuotaState.authorizesCloudExecution`, exhaustive over the enum so a new case cannot default to permitted; `canUsePCC` and `ModelExecutionReceipt.nonAuthorizingQuotaStates` both derive from it rather than keeping the second hardcoded copy that let them diverge. - **[Orchestration]** **On macOS there was no foreground check at all, so a backgrounded Shortcut could reach Private Cloud Compute with nobody present to consent.** `ModelExecutionPlanner` gates cloud on `isForegroundInteractive || consentGranted`, and `RAGService` computed that input inside `#if canImport(UIKit)` with the `#else` branch hardcoding `true`. iOS was covered; the Mac reported itself foreground-interactive unconditionally, which is precisely the case the guard was written to prevent. macOS now reads `NSApplication.shared.isActive`, the AppKit equivalent of the `UIApplication.applicationState == .active` check, and a platform with no UI framework at all falls closed to `false` so remembered consent becomes the only thing that can authorise cloud. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: RAGService.swift makePostRetrievalModelPlan constraints, ModelExecutionPlanner.swift]`

A `defer` now backstops both explicit calls, which are retained because they release the indicator when streaming ends rather than when the enclosing function returns, and that function can go on to run a continuation pass. Separately, `reportLLMToken(tokenLatencyMs:)` had **no call sites anywhere in the repository**, so `totalLLMTokensGenerated` and `lastLLMTokenLatencyMs` were permanently zero and the Neural Engine bar did not move while an answer was being written, the one moment the HUD exists to show. It is now driven from the real snapshot loop. The embedding path was already correctly instrumented and is unchanged. - **[General]** **The benchmark graded the verifier's explanation instead of the model's answer, for the second time.** `strip_verification_banner` exists because the `[Needs Verification]` wrapper contains "could not" and so tripped the abstention regex, scoring every wrapped-but-correct answer as a refusal; that cost 15 points of measured accuracy and was fixed by stripping the banner. The strip only ever handled a **well-formed** `*(Reason: …)*`, and the engine delivers reasons whose closing `)*` is absent, a literal at `SourceOnlyAnswerService.swift:349` that does not survive to the answer the harness receives, by a route not yet identified. When the closing marker is missing the optional group fails, the entire reason survives into the graded text, and the abstention regex fires on the verifier's prose. On the 2026-08-09 run `multi_hop_project_m1` named the right owner and the right deadline, hit 2 of 2 required patterns, and was scored wrong on the words "does not specify" inside its own reason; `multi_hop_project_m4` leaked identically and survived only because its reason happened to read "All necessary claims are supported by the evidence." Grading that depends on the wording of a diagnostic is not grading. The closing marker is now optional and the reason is matched to end-of-string rather than end-of-line, so multi-line and truncated reasons strip alike. Re-scoring the saved `reports/` with no rebuild and no re-run moves 2026-08-09 from 17/20 to **18/20** and reproduces the independently recorded 14/20 -> **17/20** regrade of the 2026-08-08 baseline exactly, which is what shows the change corrects rather than merely inflates. Direction is preserved where it matters: a genuine abstention inside a banner is still detected, so a correct refusal on a negative control cannot become a false hallucination. **The app-side defect is untouched and users see the malformed banner too.** `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: run_quality_matrix.py VERIFICATION_BANNER, re-score of BenchmarkRuns/20260808-retrieval-stages and 20260809-191737-matrix, 9/9 edge cases]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Every table in every Word document was parsed into rows and then dropped on the floor.** `extractTextFromWordXML` lifts each `<w:tbl>` out of the XML, leaves a bare `[[TABLE_n]]` marker where it was, extracts the paragraphs, and re-inserts the table text at that marker. The step in the middle builds its output **exclusively** from `<w:p>...</w:p>` matches, and in OOXML a `<w:tbl>` is a *sibling* of `<w:p>` inside `<w:body>`, never nested inside one, so the marker sat outside every paragraph match, never entered the result string, and the re-insertion pass found nothing to replace. The rows were computed, held in a local array, and discarded. The failure was invisible from the outside because the prose around the table came through normally, so a spreadsheet-style Word document ingested to a plausible-looking result with all of its data missing. The marker is now wrapped in a `<w:p>` so it lands in the one stream that step actually reads, at the table's original position. Marker numbering also moved from *append* order to document order in the same change, and that part is readability rather than a second defect: matches are replaced back-to-front, so append order labelled the last table 0 and stored its text at index 0 too. The pairing was reversed and internally consistent, and would have resolved correctly once substitution worked. Document order is still worth having, because the marker number now matches the table's position on the page when this has to be read out of a log. Found on the first execution of the new ingestion fixtures; no test that existed before could open a `.docx`. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentProcessor.extractTextFromWordXML, IngestionFormatCoverageTests testDOCX_PreservesTableRowsAndProse]`
- **[Ingestion]** **A fully scanned PDF told the user it had used OCR on zero pages.** The primary structured path hardcoded `usedOCR: false`. It renders and recognises a page image whether or not the PDF carries a text layer, so "Vision ran" is not the signal that OCR was needed, "there was no usable native text" is. The consequence was exactly inverted from the user's point of view: a scan that `RecognizeDocumentsRequest` read cleanly reported **0** OCR pages, while a harder one that fell through to the region-rescue path reported 1. "OCR: N pages scanned" in Document Details therefore under-reported precisely the documents where recognition did all the work. `usedOCR` is now `isGarbled || bestAvailableText.isEmpty`. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentProcessor.swift structured PageParseResult, IngestionFormatCoverageTests testScannedPDF_RecoversTableStructureThroughOCR]`
- **[Ingestion]** **iWork extraction has never worked for any document current iWork produces, and now says so.** Recorded rather than fixed, because the finding is that `extractTextFromIWorkDocument` cannot reach the content by construction: it inspects **directories only**, so a single-file `.pages`, what Pages on iOS always writes, never gets as far as a content read; and for the package form it looks for members with extension `xml` or `txt`, while modern iWork stores content as compressed protobuf in `Index/*.iwa`. Both paths end at `throw DocumentProcessingError.iWorkExtractionFailed`, which is the acceptable half of the answer: it fails loudly, so nothing is indexed as a silent empty. Two tests pin both shapes so a loud failure cannot quietly become a silent one. `.pages`, `.numbers` and `.keynote` should be treated as unsupported-but-honest rather than supported. `[evidence_level: code_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: DocumentProcessor.swift:8465]`
- **[UI]** **Restored "TinyBERT", which was removed on a false premise, and corrected the reasoning that removed it.** An earlier entry in this same `[Unreleased]` block recorded that "TinyBERT reranker" named "an architecture the app cannot verify about its own asset", because `ReRankerModel.mlpackage/Manifest.json` declares no model family. That check was necessary but not sufficient: a Core ML `.mlpackage` manifest is a packaging descriptor listing item authors and filenames, and it never carries the source architecture, so its silence is not evidence of anything. `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md` binds `cross-encoder/ms-marco-TinyBERT-L2-v2` (Apache 2.0, "Used for: on-device reranking") to `OpenIntelligence/Resources/MLModels/ReRankerModel.mlpackage` by exact path. Attribution recorded to satisfy a license is the strongest provenance in the repository, since getting it wrong is a licensing exposure rather than a copy error. The Settings entry reads "TinyBERT cross-encoder scores query-document relevance" again, with a comment naming the notices file so the next audit checks it before touching the string. The general lesson is worth keeping: absence of evidence in one artifact is not evidence of absence, and the fix for an unverified claim is to look harder before deleting it. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md:10-15, ReRankerModel.mlpackage/Manifest.json, SettingsView.swift]`
- **[General]** **Removed two unmeasured multipliers from the public-facing history.** `1000x` (RAG deduplication) and `240x` (dashboard scrolling) appeared in `CHANGELOG.md`, `Docs/RELEASE_NOTES.md`, `Docs/USER_CHANGELOG.md`, `WHATS_NEW.md`, and as Notion roadmap row *titles*, which are published publicly. No benchmark supporting either exists anywhere in the repository. Both underlying changes are real and are now described by mechanism: O(N) Set-based dedup replacing O(N²) array scans, and a UUID string cache replacing per-row recomputation. Historical entries are corrected in place with a dated note rather than deleted, so the record shows what was claimed and when it was withdrawn. This closes the last surfaces still carrying the class of figure removed from the Settings UI in this same release. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: repo-wide multiplier sweep, Notion roadmap rows]`
- **[Orchestration]** **Oversized prompts stayed on-device precisely when they could have escalated to Private Cloud Compute.** `FoundationModelRoutePolicy.determineRoute` returns from the `ModelExecutionPlan` when one is attached, and that plan is built with exact `model.tokenCount(for:)` budgets. Without a plan (the `"direct"` case in `LLMService` telemetry) it fell through to a hardcoded `onDeviceLimit = 4096` compared against a character estimate, and both halves of that comparison were wrong. Both `LLMService` sites computed the estimate as `estimateTokens(..., isAppleFMOnDevice: !config.allowPrivateCloudCompute)`. That flag selects the chars/token ratio, 1.4 on-device against 2.5 for cloud, so allowing PCC selected the *cloud* ratio and produced a token count roughly 44% below the on-device figure, which was then compared against an on-device limit. The escalation check was therefore least likely to fire in exactly the case where escalation was possible, and the number deciding the destination was derived from the assumed destination. The estimate is now unconditionally on-device, because the question being asked is "does this fit locally", not "where will this run". Separately, the limit now reads `SystemLanguageModel.default.contextSize` through `FoundationModelTokenBudget.contextSize(isAppleFMOnDevice:)` instead of assuming 4096; that helper falls back to the same 4096, so behavior on a device reporting the old window is unchanged and a device reporting a larger one can now use it. This is consistent with the device log showing 4521 tokens submitted against a 4096 window, where roughly 6,300 characters estimate to about 2,532 tokens under the cloud ratio and stay local, but that log has not been confirmed to have taken the planless path, so it is offered as the likely mechanism rather than a proven one. `estimatedTokens` also reaches `FoundationModelErrorMapper` as diagnostic metadata only, so the tightened estimate improves those receipts without changing control flow. Note this path has no test coverage; the change is build- and suite-verified but not verified on device. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+test_verified, confidence: exact_for_the_defect, unverified_on_device_for_the_resulting_route_changes, evidence_source: LLMService.swift:577/919, FoundationModelRoutePolicy.swift:28/60, FoundationModelTokenBudget.swift:28-40, ModelExecutionPlan.swift:229]`
- **[Retrieval]** **BM25 column weights were shifted one slot left, silently dropping section paths out of ranking.** `SQLiteFullTextService` ranks chunk-level FTS5 hits with an explicit weight vector, and its own comment states the intent: `section_title(10.0), section_path(5.0), content(1.0)`. The `chunks` table declares nine columns, but all four call sites passed eight weights, so every weight landed on the column before its target. `section_path` inherited the `0` meant for `structure_type` and stopped contributing to ranking entirely; `section_title` inherited the `5.0` meant for `section_path`, so the documented 10x heading boost was actually 5x; `content` ran off the end of the vector. Reproduced in `sqlite3` against a copy of the real schema before changing anything: on a corpus where the query matched a section path, that chunk scored `0.0` and sorted *below* a chunk that merely mentioned the term in body text, where the intended weights rank it second. Two of the misaligned slots landed on `UNINDEXED` columns and were inert either way. Fixed by passing one weight per column and pinning the column list in a comment beside the vector. The git history is squashed into public-repo snapshots, so when the ninth column was added relative to the vector is not recoverable and no cause is claimed. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+reproduced_in_sqlite3, confidence: exact, evidence_source: SQLiteFullTextService.swift:242-254, :1448-1461]`
- **[Chunking]** **The chunker stopped calling itself "Late Chunking", which is a different technique it does not implement.** The container settings sheet told users "Documents are split using embedding-based boundary detection (Late Chunking)", and `SemanticChunker` carried the same label in six places. Late chunking (Günther et al.) embeds the *entire document* through a long-context model and mean-pools the resulting token vectors within each chunk span, so every chunk vector is computed with full-document context. `SemanticChunker.detectEmbeddingBoundaries` embeds individual *sentences*, compares adjacent pairs by cosine similarity, and returns `[String.Index]` boundary positions; chunks are then embedded independently and carry no cross-chunk context. The sentence describing the actual mechanism was already accurate and is unchanged, so only the technique name is removed. This is the same class of claim as the "TinyBERT reranker" string corrected above: a named architecture the code does not implement. The file header now states the distinction explicitly so the label does not propagate back into UI copy, and real late chunking remains a roadmap row. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: SemanticChunker.swift:1104-1137, ContainerSettingsSheet+Sections.swift:44]`
- **[UI]** **The app no longer advertises two features it does not have.** The Settings capability list, which tells the user what the *active* quality mode is doing right now, included "Dynamic Profiles, mid-session model swapping for planning, grounding, and execution" and "Model Judges, Cloud Pro evaluates Core Advanced outputs for maximum accuracy". `FoundationModelDynamicProfileRegistry` has **zero call sites** outside its own file, and there is no judge implementation anywhere in `Services/`. Neither runs, in any mode. A list captioned as live behavior is not a place for roadmap items, so both rows are removed rather than reworded, and each is tracked on the roadmap for re-adding when it actually executes. "Native Spotlight Integration, hooks directly into Apple Core Spotlight for OS-level retrieval" was the same overclaim in weaker form: `SpotlightIndexService` genuinely populates the Core Spotlight index, but the system Search tool is not bound into `FoundationModelToolRegistry`, so nothing retrieves through it. It now reads "Spotlight Indexing, your documents are indexed into Apple Core Spotlight", which is what the code does. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: SettingsView.swift, FoundationModelDynamicProfileRegistry.swift, EngineSDKCompatibility.swift]`
- **[UI]** **The model picker stops naming a model tier the app cannot select.** `LLMModelType.displayName` returned `"AFM 3 Core Advanced"` for the Apple Intelligence target, and that string surfaces in pickers and diagnostics. The public SDK exposes no tier selector, and `SystemLanguageModel.default` is what executes on every device, so the name asserted a model the app can neither choose nor observe. Now `"Apple Intelligence"`. This is the same correction as the 3B/20B capability-card removal in 4.7, which this string survived because that pass changed the card and not the display name. `[evidence_level: sdk_verified+code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: LLMModelType.swift]`
- **[General]** **Unmeasured performance figures no longer ship, including inside the corpus the app cites back to users.** Three surfaces asserted numbers that physical-device measurement contradicts. The Settings capability card claimed `< 0.8 s` median TTFT and `≈65 tok/s`; measured A18 Pro figures are 2.2–3.2 s and 27 tok/s on-device, 86 tok/s on PCC, making the throughput claim roughly 2.4× optimistic and the latency claim unsupported. Both now read the measured range, each labelled `A18 Pro` so the figure carries the hardware it came from rather than implying a fleet-wide number. The container settings sheet's `PT-MoE 32K` badge is gone; the public SDK exposes neither the server model's architecture nor its context window, and `32768` is a hardcoded fallback whose own comment directs routing callers to the live capability provider. It now reads `Native PCC (Apple Intelligence)`, parallel to the on-device branch beside it.

  **The corpus mattered more than the chrome.** `SampleDocumentManager` ships the documents new users are told to import first, so the engine retrieves them and cites them back as sourced fact. Those documents described the on-device path as "zero-latency" against a measured 2.2–3.2 s TTFT, and asserted a "32K" PCC window in five places. All corrected to the measured figures, with the PCC window described as system-reported rather than fixed by this app. Two further `32K` assertions in `SettingsView` are also gone. Remaining `32768` occurrences are the code constant and its comments, which are accurate as a described fallback. `[evidence_level: measured+code_verified+build_verified, confidence: high_for_this_device, evidence_source: LLMModelType.swift, ContainerSettingsSheet+Sections.swift, SampleDocumentManager.swift, SettingsView.swift, device runs 2026-07-30]`
- **[General]** **The QA suite stopped grading the engine on repeating an unverified claim.** `QualityAssuranceService` case `pcc_token_window` asked "What is the Private Cloud Compute context window?" with `expectedAnswer: "32K tokens"` and `expectedDocumentIds: ["ragtechnicalarchitecture"]`, the app's own sample document, and the same unverified figure the entry above removes from it. The case is deleted rather than corrected, because correcting it would preserve the circularity: a test whose ground truth is the app's own documentation cannot distinguish "the engine answered badly" from "the engine faithfully repeated a stale document," and it would now fail against the corrected sample text regardless. Replacement fixtures with external ground truth are tracked on the roadmap. Note the committed test corpus cannot currently support that work: `Docs/TestDocuments/` is six small ingestion edge cases testing no answer quality, and `rag_validation_sample.json` points at a gitignored PDF, so the only quality corpus this repository references is not in it. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: QualityAssuranceService.swift]`

- **[UI]** **Settings named eight agentic tools, and all eight were the unregistered ones.** The mode-capability list advertised "8 Tool Functions: SearchDocs, ListDocs, GetSummary, CountPattern, ExactSearch, Stats, Related, Compare". `FoundationModelToolRegistry.createTools` is the only place tools are handed to a `LanguageModelSession` (single call site at `FoundationModelSessionFactory.swift:32`), and it registers four: `RetrieveCorpusEvidenceTool`, `InspectDocumentTool`, `CompareTopicAcrossDocumentsTool`, `GetLibraryOverviewTool`. The eight named in the UI are declared in the same file and referenced nowhere else in the codebase, confirmed per-type. So the list named the entire dead set and none of the live one, almost certainly because the copy was written from the v4.0 roadmap row and never revisited when the toolset was replaced. Now names the four that run, with a comment tying the list to `createTools` rather than to the struct count. The eight dead structs are left in place and tracked on the roadmap as a delete-or-register decision, since removing them is a behavior question rather than a copy fix. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: FoundationModelToolRegistry.swift, FoundationModelSessionFactory.swift, SettingsView.swift]`

- **[UI]** **Audited every remaining claim in the Settings mode-capability list.** That list is captioned to users as what the active mode is doing right now, and four more entries did not survive a check against code. **"1,000x faster evidence retrieval"** was an unmeasured multiplier with no benchmark behind it, the same class as the `≈65 tok/s` figure already removed; the O(N) change is real, the number never was, so it now describes the mechanism. **"TinyBERT reranker"** named an architecture the app cannot verify about its own asset: `ReRankerModel.mlpackage` declares no model family in its manifest, so it now reads "a cross-encoder". **"200K+ Token Budget, 50 sessions × 4K"** multiplied out to a figure that reads like a context window and is not one; Maximum runs up to 50 *separate* 4K windows and never holds 200K at once, the 50 is a ceiling whose own config comment says thermal stops it first, and 4.9 added convergence stopping precisely so it exits early. Now "Up to 50 Sessions, each gets a fresh 4K window; stops early once it converges". Entries that did check out are left alone: HyDE, 2-hop graph expansion, intent routing, RAPTOR-lite summaries, contextual compression, parent-document retrieval, and the A–I gate list. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: SettingsView.swift, AgenticConfig.unlimited, ReRankerModel.mlpackage/Manifest.json]`
- **[Orchestration]** **Confirmed Deep Think's unreachable stop threshold is fixed and correctly wired**, closing a roadmap item that had been left as "fix written, not verified". `sessionProgress` used a hardcoded `log(50.0)` denominator, right for Maximum's 50-session budget and wrong for Deep Think's 8, where it returned 0.382 and capped an 8-session run near 73% against a 90% gate. Deep Think therefore never early-exited and burned all eight sessions on every query. `calculateProgressConfidence` now takes a `sessionBudget` and scales the denominator to it. The wiring is the part worth recording: the Deep Think call site passes `sessionBudget: effectiveMaxSessions`, while `calculateRealConfidence` omits it and falls to the `= 50` default, which is correct because its only caller is `executeTrueUnlimitedReasoning`, where 50 genuinely is the budget. Maximum got a separate fix in the same release, replacing a three-way conjunction gated on an unreachable 0.98 with two independent stop conditions, `completed` and `converged`. All three commits landed before the 4.9 release commit. Device verification of the resulting early exits is still outstanding. `[evidence_level: code_verified, confidence: high_unverified_on_device, evidence_source: AgenticPolicyService.swift, AgenticOrchestrator.swift, commits 8a8c7ab/7160b8b/fa3fc5a]`
- **[General]** **The session-start hook read prose as part of a commit sha.** It parsed the `Last verified commit:` line by taking the whole remainder and stripping the whitespace out of it, so anything written after the sha became part of the sha. An explanatory clause added to that line turned the mild "HEAD has moved N commits" note into "names a commit not in this repository" — which reads like a corrupt or foreign checkout, and is the one thing a handoff document cannot afford a fresh session to read first. The parser now takes the first whitespace-delimited token, so the line may carry prose after the sha without breaking. Drift is now counted over commits that changed something other than `Docs/ai/STATE.md`, which generalises the hard-coded "exactly one behind" exemption it replaces: a handoff cannot record the sha of the commit that contains it and docs-only follow-ups land after that, so a well-maintained handoff always trailed HEAD and therefore always warned. The pathspec is anchored with `:(top)`, because `:(exclude)` otherwise resolves against the working directory and counts 4 rather than 0 when the hook is invoked from a subdirectory.
- **[General]** **The preflight reported v5.0 as already shipped, and pointed new work at the version after it.** `detect_active_release` read the first numbered heading in this file as the last shipped release. Here that heading is the *open* section: it is written when the version is bumped, and all nine commits of the v5.0 cycle filed their entries into it while `[Unreleased]` stayed empty. So the router reported `state: shipped` for a release with 28 open roadmap rows, no `v5.0` tag, and Archive/TestFlight validation still pending. `CLAUDE.md` and the `notion-roadmap` skill both instruct sessions to read that field when choosing a release target, and the skill records what a wrong one costs: a build rejected by App Store Connect on 2026-07-28. The trap had a second jaw. Adding one entry under `[Unreleased]`, which is exactly what the old `Changelog target` told you to do, flipped the router to `active release v5.1` and `Notion Target Release: v5.1` — a version that is not an option in the Notion schema at all. A `<!-- unreleased -->` marker on the heading now states the one fact nothing else in the repository records. The date cannot carry it, because it marks when the section opened; git tags cannot either, because v4.0 and v4.2 through v4.6 have none. Removing the marker is what cutting a release means. Left on a section that has shipped the router keeps naming that release, which is wrong but bounded, and self-corrects as soon as a newer section opens above it. `Changelog target` is derived from the same fact instead of being hardcoded to `[Unreleased]`.

### Changed
- **[UI]** **Settings was fifteen cards on one plane, and everything on it shouted at the same volume.** 2805 lines, six distinct corner radii, and your billing plan sitting at the same visual altitude as the GPU execution profile, simultaneously overwhelming for a new user and tedious for anyone who had to scroll past the hero panel to reach a knob. The diagnosis that matters is that this was a **depth** problem rather than a styling one: converting the fifteen cards into fifteen `Form` sections would have changed nothing about it. The root is now a native `List` of about ten `NavigationLink` rows across five sections, each pushing to a detail screen that renders the same cards untouched, plus `.searchable` over a keyword index so a knob can be found by the name it has in the code, "temperature", "chunk overlap", "top-k", rather than by the screen it happens to live on. That combination is what lets the surface stay calm without removing any of the complexity, and it is the pattern Settings.app itself uses. The hero panel shrank from a ~500pt centred card to a compact identity row for the same reason Settings.app keeps the Apple Account card small: it is the first row of an index, not a title page. A native `List` also brings correct Dynamic Type row layout, platform insets, and `Section` footers for copy that was loose `Text` inside tinted panels. `developerCard` and `aboutCard`, both `NavigationLink`-in-a-card wrappers, are deleted; their destinations are reached like every other screen now. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+simulator_verified, confidence: exact, unverified_search_input, evidence_source: SettingsView.swift, screenshots of the index, a pushed detail screen, and the list bottom on iPhone 17 Pro / iOS 27]`
- **[UI]** **Five switches in the Apple Intelligence card controlled nothing, and one of them was being counted as live capability.** `enableWritingTools`, `enableSpeechAnalysis`, `enableTranslation`, `enableScreenAwareness` and `enableADM3Visuals` each persisted to `UserDefaults` and had **zero consumers** anywhere in the app, its tests, or the Live Activities extension. Flipping any of them changed nothing. **The features themselves are real and always on**, which is why these became status rows rather than deletions: Writing Tools ships through `.writingToolsBehavior(.complete)` on the chat composer, `SpeechAnalyzerService` runs inside `DocumentProcessor` for audio and video, `TranslationService` has live call sites, the Screen Awareness App Intents are registered, and the Image Playground path is wired. Removing the claim would have withdrawn something true; removing the control removes something false. They now render with the same "Always On" treatment App Tips and Gazetteer already had. The card's badge counted seven, three of which were these dead switches, so it reported inert settings as active capability; it counts the four real toggles now. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: repo-wide grep for each key excluding SettingsStore/SettingsView, ChatComposerV2.swift:88, DocumentProcessor SpeechAnalyzerService call site]`
- **[Orchestration]** **Sampling was inferred rather than chosen, so Top-P was unreachable and greedy decoding was impossible.** `LLMService` built its `SamplingMode` with `if topK > 0 && topK < 100 { .random(top: topK) } else if topP < 1 { .random(probabilityThreshold: topP) }`, and every chat query passes a hardcoded `topK: 40`, so the first branch always won. The Top-P slider in Settings was read, persisted, threaded through `InferenceConfig`, and then discarded; `SamplingMode.greedy` could not be reached at all. Apple's three modes are alternatives rather than layers, so `InferenceConfig` now carries an explicit `SamplingStrategy` (`greedy` / `topK` / `topP`) and the UI is a picker that reveals only the parameter belonging to the selected mode. **Defaults to `.topK`, so behaviour is unchanged for anyone upgrading.** Two capabilities are newly reachable as a result: greedy decoding, and `seed`, both random modes accept one, the app never passed it, and without it the same question against the same library could not reproduce the same answer. That is worth having in a tool whose claim is grounded, auditable answers, and it is surfaced as "Reproducible answers" rather than as a raw integer. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, unverified_on_device, evidence_source: iPhoneOS.sdk FoundationModels.swiftinterface SamplingMode, LLMService.swift:672-694, ChatScreen.swift:2625, LLMModel.swift InferenceConfig]`
- **[UI]** **Making the generation controls reachable exposed three that cannot do anything, and two routing claims that were backwards.** `GenerationOptions` in the iOS 27 SDK `swiftinterface` declares exactly `sampling`/`samplingMode`, `temperature`, `maximumResponseTokens` and `toolCallingMode`; the string "penalty" does not occur anywhere in the FoundationModels interface. **Temperature and max response tokens are real**, `SettingsStore` writes the same `llmTemperature`/`llmMaxTokens` keys `ChatScreen` reads through `@AppStorage`, and both arrive in `GenerationOptions`, so they affect on-device and Private Cloud Compute answers alike. **Frequency, presence and repetition penalty are not**, and they are now in their own section saying so instead of sharing a footer that read "These parameters affect token sampling behavior". They are **not deleted**: their consumer `LocalOpenAIServerLLMService` is scaffolding for the roadmap row "Bring-your-own local model on Mac", which targets v5.0, and zero call sites is precisely what an open roadmap row means, this is the failure mode `oi-claim-audit` exists to prevent, and running it is what stopped the deletion. Separately, two copies of the same false routing claim are corrected: "Reliability-first auto routing (prefers PCC for library queries; falls back on-device)" had the direction backwards, because `FoundationModelRoutePolicy` returns `.onDevice` on every branch of the `.automatic` path unless the estimate *exceeds* the on-device window and PCC is both allowed and available, with `.exactLookup` unconditionally on-device, the device is the default and PCC is the escalation. And the context-length caption hardcoded "Apple Foundation Models use a 4,096 token context window", which is the fallback rather than the live figure; it now reads `SystemLanguageModel`'s real window and warns when the chosen size exceeds it, because the context budget is what the packing stage fills, so selecting 32K on a 4K device builds a prompt the model then rejects. Generation Tuning is dropped from Advanced, its whole content having been one `TextField` bound to the same `llmSystemPrompt` the sheet's full editor already owns. **Known and not fixed here:** Top-P is written, read, threaded through `InferenceConfig` and then discarded, because `LLMService` prefers top-K whenever it is in `(0,100)` and `ChatScreen` hardcodes `topK: 40`. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+sdk_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: iPhoneOS.sdk FoundationModels.swiftinterface GenerationOptions, FoundationModelRoutePolicy.swift:49-110, LLMService.swift:673-692, SettingsStore.swift:65-70]`
- **[UI]** **Every shipped release is browsable in Settings, rendered from the changelog rather than a second copy of it.** The post-update sheet only ever fires for someone who updated, and stays deliberately silent on a fresh install, a list of fixes to problems you never hit is noise on day one and competes with onboarding for the same moment. That leaves nothing for a user who wants to judge whether the app is actually maintained, which is what a version history answers. **Settings -> Version History** reads `VersionHistory.md` out of the app bundle and parses it into collapsible releases, newest expanded. The bundled file is a copy of `Docs/USER_CHANGELOG.md`, which the repository already requires to be updated whenever user-visible behaviour changes, so the in-app history cannot fall behind the written one and every future release updates it for free. **The copy exists only because the app cannot read a file outside its bundle and a copy build phase would mean editing hard-boundary `project.pbxproj`**, `OpenIntelligence/Resources` is a `PBXFileSystemSynchronizedRootGroup`, so dropping the file there bundles it with no project change, verified by finding it in the built `.app`. Duplication is how claims in this repository have drifted before, so `VersionHistoryTests` fails the suite if the two copies diverge, and separately asserts that every `## ` heading survives parsing, the failure that matters is a heading shape the parser does not recognise silently dropping a whole release. The parser handles only the shapes the file actually uses, counted across all eleven releases: 119 `*   **Bold lead.** Remainder` bullets, ~19 plain ones, `### ` subheadings and `> ` notes. Anything else is skipped rather than guessed at. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified+test_verified+simulator_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: VersionHistory.swift, VersionHistoryTests 3/3, screenshots of the row and the rendered history on iPhone 17 Pro / iOS 27]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Any automatic import landed in whichever library happened to be selected.** `enqueueDocuments` read `containerService.activeContainerId` and stamped it onto every queued item, with no way to override it. That is right for a user pressing Add Document and wrong for anything the app decides to import on its own: opening Documents with "Library 2" selected while a sample refresh was pending would have deleted the samples out of General and deposited them in Library 2, because `ragService.documents` spans every container so the refresh finds them regardless of which library is on screen. `enqueueDocuments` and `ingestDocuments` now take an optional `containerId` that still defaults to the active library, and the sample refresh passes an explicit one: the container the stale documents are actually in, falling back to `containers.first`, which is the same rule `documentsForContainer` uses to decide that a `nil` `containerId` means the default library. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: RAGService.swift:4394-4403 and :4430, SampleDocumentManager.refreshStaleSamples]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Correcting a sample document did nothing for anyone who already had it, so the samples now refresh themselves and say why.** The bodies are Swift literals written to disk at import time; `writeSamplesToDocumentsDirectory` rewrites a file only when the text differs, so the *file* updated but the ingested chunks and vectors did not. Every user who onboarded before v5.0 was still being told, by their own library and as sourced fact, that the app reads Pages/Numbers/Keynote and that a "Telemetry HUD" screen exists. Each descriptor now carries a SHA-256 of its body, the hash actually ingested is recorded per sample, and a mismatch triggers a replace-in-place on the next visit to Documents. **Removing before re-importing is load-bearing, not tidiness:** managed storage uniquifies a colliding filename by appending `-2` (`WorkspaceSyncService`, counter starting at 2) and that rename happens *before* the duplicate check compares paths, so importing over the top produces `OpenIntelligence-Product-Guide-2.md` beside the original instead of replacing it, which is exactly the duplication the owner hit when replaying onboarding. `removeDocument(keepPhysicalFile: false)` clears the managed file first, so the re-import lands on the original name. Guarded so it never re-adds a sample the user deleted on purpose, and never runs while onboarding is importing those same files. Because a pre-v5.0 install has no recorded hash at all, the `nil` case correctly reads as stale and refreshes once. A banner then explains what happened and why, since three documents vanishing and reappearing with the ingestion theater running would otherwise read as a bug. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, unverified_on_device, evidence_source: SampleDocumentManager staleImportedSamples/refreshStaleSamples, RAGService.swift:6854 and :6874, WorkspaceSyncService.swift:3293-3298]`
- **[Ingestion]** **Auditing the other two sample documents found four more claims the code does not support, one of them introduced by the fix immediately below.** These files are ingested and cited back as sourced fact, so each is the app asserting something false about itself. **"The app reads 50+ formats"** survived in the RAG document after being removed from the Product Guide, leaving the corpus contradicting itself; it now points at the one list rather than restating a count. **Embedding was attributed to Accelerate "running on the GPU and Neural Engine"**, in two documents, and that conflates three different things: vectors are produced by a Core ML sentence model on the Neural Engine, Accelerate (vDSP/BLAS) is CPU SIMD used to *compare* vectors afterwards, and Metal shaders handle the bulk similarity, a split Settings already describes correctly at `SettingsView.swift:1687`. **A whole "Telemetry HUD" section described a surface that does not exist as written**, instructing users to "tap the status indicator during any query" to see route, token budget and pathway; the Silicon HUD is an Appearance toggle showing live CPU/ANE/GPU activity, and route and token budget live in the metrics bar under each answer. Two separate surfaces had been merged into one imaginary one. And **the on-device window was stated as a fixed 4,096** when `FoundationModelTokenBudget.contextSize` asks the system and only falls back to that number, so it now says the app reads the real window and names 4,096 as the figure on current hardware. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: SampleDocumentManager.swift, CoreMLSentenceEmbeddingProvider.swift:117-118, SettingsView.swift:1687 and :2929, FoundationModelTokenBudget.contextSize]`
- **[Ingestion]** **The sample corpus told users the app reads Pages, Numbers and Keynote.** Those three sat in the format list of `SampleDocumentManager`, which is not chrome: those documents are ingested, retrieved and cited back as sourced fact, so the app was answering "yes, it supports Pages" out of its own library while `extractTextFromIWorkDocument` threw `iWorkExtractionFailed` on every such file, a defect this same release documents and pins with two tests. The list now stops at the formats that work and states plainly that iWork files will fail on import and should be exported first, which matches what the picker actually does: it still offers `.pages`, `.numbers` and `.key`, so the failure is reachable and worth naming rather than hiding. The count "50+ file formats" went with them, because removing three from an enumerated list makes an asserted total wrong and nothing in the repository measures it. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: SampleDocumentManager.swift format list, DocumentProcessor.swift:8498, DocumentPicker.swift:214]`
- **[UI]** **The hardware X-ray outlines turned with the interface instead of staying over the chip.** `orientRect` transforms the component rectangles correctly for each interface orientation, but it read orientation from a computed property that went straight to `scene.effectiveGeometry.interfaceOrientation`. SwiftUI had no dependency to invalidate on, so nothing re-rendered the overlay when the device turned, and reading that value mid-transition returns the pre-rotation orientation anyway. The result defeated the entire premise of the feature: the outlines mark where the Neural Engine, GPU and CPU physically sit on the board, which is a fixed place that does not move when the UI rotates. Orientation is now `@State`, refreshed from `geometry.size` changes, those land after the rotation settles, which is when the scene reports the correct value, and it avoids the `beginGeneratingDeviceOrientationNotifications` bookkeeping that `UIDevice.orientationDidChangeNotification` would need. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, unverified_on_device, evidence_source: MotherboardHUDView.swift orientRect, resolveInterfaceOrientation, HardwareXRayOverlay geometry reader]`
- **[UI]** **The onboarding pipeline log rendered text on top of text, and the fix that made ingestion fast is what exposed it.** `visible` is a sliding six-row window over a list that only grows, so every append changes which ids are on screen: one row leaves the top as another enters the bottom. Each row carried `.transition(.opacity.combined(with: .move(edge: .bottom)))` inside a plain `VStack` animated on `logEntries.count`, so a departing row and an arriving row animated through the same space. At eighty seconds per import each stage crawled and the overlap was brief enough to miss; at **7.8 seconds** entries arrive in bursts and several rows were mid-transition at once, which is when it became unreadable. A log ticker does not need animated reflow, so the per-row transition and the container animation are gone and the stack is clipped so a row cannot paint outside the panel mid-update. The page also gained the `ScrollView` treatment page 1 received earlier, for the same reason: a paged `TabView` fixes its page height, this page overflowed it, and the title was clipped at the top while the completion card's last example question was clipped at the bottom. And the heading "Watch your library come online" is now "Building your library", becoming "Your library is ready" on completion, because the old one named a loading screen rather than what the user is looking at. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: OnboardingChecklistView.swift logTickerView and pipelineTheaterPage, owner device screenshots]`
- **[UI]** **The Silicon HUD looked like decoration and never said it could be moved.** It starts life over the navigation bar, which is where the owner first saw it colliding with content, and nothing in the app explained that the floating readout is live telemetry from the chip. A one-time hint is now attached to the legend itself rather than positioned on screen, so it travels with the readout when dragged and cannot end up pointing at empty space. It retires permanently the first time the legend is actually moved and placed, which is the action it asks for. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, unverified_on_device, evidence_source: MotherboardHUDView.swift SiliconLegend, clampAndPersist]`
- **[Orchestration]** **The Top-K slider wrote to nothing, and the value the model actually used was hardcoded.** Making sampling selectable earlier in this release fixed `LLMService`, which now switches properly on `samplingStrategy`, but left the number itself stranded: `SettingsStore` had no `topK` key, `applySettings()` never wrote one, and `ChatScreen` passed a literal `40` into `InferenceConfig`. So a user could pick Balanced, drag the slider to 15, tap Apply, and generation would continue at 40 forever. A `llmTopK` key now carries it end to end, defaulting to 40 so behaviour is unchanged until the slider is moved. Worth recording why this hid for so long: the two numbers called "top-K" in this codebase are unrelated. One is the sampling shortlist; the other is how many chunks retrieval returns, and they sit in the same `query(_:topK:config:)` call, so the hardcoded literal read as deliberate. Both declarations now say which is which. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: ChatScreen.swift:2631, SettingsStore.swift Keys.topK, ModelConfigurationSheet applySettings/loadCurrentSettings]`

There was nothing to cancel and nothing that would have cancelled it, so after N queries the run loop woke 5N times a second for the rest of the session, beneath a comment reading "only connected during processing to save battery". The connection is retained now and cancelled both when processing ends and on disappear. `[evidence_level: code_verified+build_verified, confidence: exact, evidence_source: ChatScreen.swift:568-574]`

Measured beats guessed. **Correcting an overstatement made while diagnosing this:** `hasActiveCooling` has exactly one call site and it is display-only, so this was never the sustained-load ceiling it was first described as; the GPU ceiling is `maxSafeGPUAccelerationLevel`, which returns 1.0 for all Macs, and the adaptive optimizer throttles against live thermal state independently.
